圣彼得太太快步下楼去给他调一杯鸡尾酒。她觉出他身上有一种异乎寻常的疲惫，几乎能感觉得到他舌尖上那股苦涩。她知道，一个男人能从自己女儿那里受到一种别样的伤害——那是人之为人所能遭受的最残酷的伤害之一。她的心为戈弗雷作痛。

===Original===
When the Professor had been warmed and comforted by a good dinner, he lit a cigar and sat down before the hearth to read. After a while his wife saw that the book had slid to his knee, and he was looking into the fire. Studying his dark profile, she noticed that the corners of his funny eyebrows rose, as if he were amused by something.

===Chinese===
一顿丰盛的晚餐让教授暖和过来、也舒坦了之后，他点起一支雪茄，在壁炉前坐下看书。过了一会儿，妻子看见那本书已滑落到他膝头，他正凝望着炉火。她端详着他黝黑的侧脸，注意到他那两道古怪的眉毛，眉梢微微扬起，仿佛被什么事逗乐了。

===Original===
“What are you thinking about, Godfrey?” she said presently. “Just then you were smiling—quite agreeably!”
“I was thinking,” he answered absently, “about Euripides; how, when he was an old man, he went and lived in a cave by the sea, and it was thought queer, at the time. It seems that houses had become insupportable to him. I wonder whether it was because he had observed women so closely all his life.”

===Chinese===
「你在想什么呢，戈弗雷？」过了一会儿她说道。「刚才你在笑——笑得还挺舒坦！」
「我在想，」他心不在焉地答道，「欧里庇得斯；他老了以后，跑到海边的一个洞穴里去住，那时人都觉得这事古怪。看来，是屋子再也让他受不了了。我不知道，这是不是因为他这一辈子把女人观察得太细的缘故。」

### XV / 第十五

===Original===
The month of March was the dreariest and bleakest of the year in Hamilton, and Louie strove to brighten it by opening a discussion of plans for the summer. He had been hinting for some time that he had a very attractive project up his sleeve, and though he had not succeeded in keeping it from Mrs. St. Peter, he said nothing to the Professor until one night when they were dining at the Marselluses’. All through dinner Louie kept reminding them of the specialties of this and that Paris restaurant, so that St. Peter was not altogether unprepared.
As they left the dining-room, Louie burst out with it. He and Rosamond were to take Doctor and Mrs. St. Peter to France for the summer. Louie had decided upon the dates, the boat, the itinerary; he was intoxicated with the pleasure of planning.
“Understand,” he said, “it is to be our excursion, from Hamilton back to Hamilton. We’ll travel in the most ample comfort, but not in magnificence. We’ll go down to Biarritz for a little fashionable life, and stop at Marseilles to see your foster-brother, Charles Thierault. The rest of the summer we’ll lead a scholarly life in Paris. I have my own reasons for wishing you to go along, Professor. The pleasure of your company would be quite enough, but I have also other reasons. I want to see the intellectual side of Paris, and to meet some of the savants and men of letters whom you know. What a shame Gaston Paris is not living! We could very nicely make up a little party at Lapérouse for him. But there are others.”

===Chinese===
三月是汉密尔顿一年里最阴沉、最萧瑟的月份，路易便提出商量夏天的计划，好给这日子添点亮色。他暗示了些时日，说袖子里藏着一个很叫人动心的方案；虽说瞒不过圣彼得太太，他对教授却一直没开口，直到有一天晚上，他们在马塞勒斯家赴宴。整顿晚餐，路易不住地提起巴黎这家那家馆子的拿手菜，圣彼得心里也就有了几分底。
一离开餐厅，路易便脱口而出。他和罗莎蒙德要带圣彼得博士和太太去法国过夏天。日期、轮船、行程，路易都已定好；他沉浸在筹划的乐趣里，简直像醉了一般。
「要知道，」他说，「这是我们的一次出游，从汉密尔顿出发，再回汉密尔顿。我们要以最宽裕的舒适旅行，却不必奢华。我们要南下比亚里茨过一点时髦生活，在马赛停一停，去看您的义兄夏尔·蒂罗。余下的夏天，我们就在巴黎过学者的日子。教授，我有我自己的理由盼望您同志。能与您同行，这快乐便足够了，可我还有别的理由。我想见识见识巴黎知识界的一面，见见您认识的那些学者和文坛中人。加斯东·帕里斯（Gaston Paris）不在人世了，真可惜！否则我们满可以在拉佩鲁兹为他组一个小小的饭局。不过也还有别的人。」

===Original===
Mrs. St. Peter developed the argument. “Yes, Louie, you and Godfrey can lunch with the scholars while Rosamond and I are shopping.”
Marsellus looked alarmed. “Not at all, Dearest! It’s to be understood that I always shop with you. I adore the shops in Paris. Besides, we shall want you with us when we lunch with celebrities. When was a savant, and a Frenchman, not eager for the company of two charming ladies at déjeuner? And you may have too much of the society of your sposi; very nice for you to have variety. You must keep a little engagement book: Lundi, déjeuner, M. Emile Faguet. Mercredi, diner, M. Anatole France; and so on.”
St. Peter chuckled. “I’m afraid you exaggerate the circumference of my social circle, Louie. I haven’t the pleasure of knowing Anatole France.”
“No matter; we can have M. Paul Bourget for Wednesday.”

===Chinese===
圣彼得太太把这话接过去发挥。「好哇，路易，你和戈弗雷跟学者们去吃午饭，我和罗莎蒙德好去买东西。」
马塞勒斯一脸惊惶。「决不，亲爱的！得说定了，我总要陪你去买东西。我热爱巴黎那些铺子。再说，我们跟名流吃饭的时候，也少不了你们。哪个学者，哪个法国人，在午餐（déjeuner）上不愿有两位迷人的女士作陪呢？再说，老是和自己的丈夫（sposi）在一起，也会腻的；换换花样，对你们大有好处。你得预备一本小小的约会簿：星期一，午餐，埃米尔·法格先生；星期三，晚餐，阿纳托尔·法朗士先生；以此类推。」
圣彼得轻声笑了。「恐怕你把我的交际圈子夸大了，路易。阿纳托尔·法朗士（Anatole France），我可没有结识的荣幸。」
「不打紧；星期三我们可以请保罗·布尔热（Paul Bourget）先生。」

===Original===
“You can help us, too, about finding things for the house, Papa,” said Rosamond. “We expect to pick up a good many things. The Thieraults ought to know good shops down in the South, where prices have not gone up.”
“I’m afraid the antiquaries are centralized in Paris. I never saw anything very interesting in Lyons or the Midi. However, they may exist.”
“Charles Thierault is still interested in a shipping-line that runs to the City of Mexico, isn’t he? He could perfectly well send our purchases from Marseilles to the City of Mexico for us. They would go in without duty, and Louie thinks he can get them across the border as household goods.”
“That sounds practicable, Rosie. It might be managed.”
Marsellus laughed and patted his wife’s hand. “Oh-ho, cher Papa, you haven’t begun to find how practical we can be!”
“Well, Louie, it’s a tempting idea, and I’ll think it over. I’ll see whether I can arrange my work.” St. Peter knew at that moment that he would never be one of this lighthearted expedition, and he hated himself for the ungracious drawing-back that he felt in the region of his diaphragm.

===Chinese===
「你也能帮我们给家里找些东西呀，爸爸，」罗莎蒙德说，「我们打算淘不少物件。蒂罗家在南方那边该认识些好铺子，价钱还没涨起来的。」
「恐怕古玩商都集中在巴黎。我在里昂或南方从没见过什么顶有趣的东西。不过，也许也有。」
「夏尔·蒂罗不是还在一家开往墨西哥城的航运公司里有股份么？他完全可以把我们的东西从马赛运到墨西哥城，替我们发过去。那样进来免关税，路易觉得他能想法子把它们当作家用物品运过边境。」
「这倒听来行得通，罗西。也许能办成。」
马塞勒斯笑了，轻轻拍了拍妻子的手。「嗬嗬，好爸爸（cher Papa），您还没见识过我们能有多精明呢！」
「好吧，路易，这是个诱人的主意，我考虑考虑。看看我的工作能否安排得开。」圣彼得那一刻便知道，自己永远不会加入这趟快活的远行，他心里感到一阵退却——就在横膈膜那一带——又因这退却的不近人情而恨着自己。

===Original===
The family discussed their summer plans all evening. Louie wanted to write at once for rooms at the Meurice, but Mrs. St. Peter ruled it out as too expensive.
That night, after he was in bed, St. Peter tried in vain to justify himself in his inevitable refusal. He liked Paris, and he liked Louie. But one couldn’t do one’s own things in another person’s way; selfish or not, that was the truth. Besides, he would not be needed. He could trust Louie to take every care of Lillian, and nobody could please her more than her son-in-law. Beaux-fils, apparently, were meant by Providence to take the husband’s place when husbands had ceased to be lovers. Marsellus never forgot one of the hundred foolish little attentions that Lillian loved. Best of all, he admired her extravagantly, her distinction was priceless to him. Many people admired her, but Louie more than most. That worldliness, that willingness to get the most out of occasions and people, which had developed so strongly in Lillian in the last few years, seemed to Louie as natural and proper as it seemed unnatural to Godfrey. It was an element that had always been in Lillian, and as long as it resulted in mere fastidiousness, was not a means to an end, St. Peter had liked it, too. He knew it was due to this worldliness, even more than to the fact that his wife had a little money of her own, that she and his daughters had never been drab and a little pathetic, like some of the faculty women. They hadn’t much, but they were never absurd. They never made shabby compromises. If they couldn’t get the right thing, they went without. Usually they had the right thing, and it got paid for, somehow. He couldn’t say they were extravagant; the old house had been funny and bare enough, but there were no ugly things in it.
Since Rosamond’s marriage to Marsellus, both she and her mother had changed bewilderingly in some respects—changed and hardened. But Louie, who had done the damage, had not damaged himself. It was to him that one appealed—for Augusta, for Professor Crane, for the bruised feelings of people less fortunate. It was less because of Louie than for any other reason that he would refuse this princely invitation.
He could get out of it without hurting anybody—though he knew Louie would be sorry. He could simply insist that he must work, and that he couldn’t work away from his old study. There were some advantages about being a writer of histories. The desk was a shelter one could hide behind, it was a hole one could creep into.

===Chinese===
全家人一晚上都在讨论夏天的计划。路易想马上写信去订默里斯酒店的房间，圣彼得太太否决了——太贵。
那天夜里上了床之后，圣彼得想为自己那不可避免的拒绝找个名正言顺的理由，却徒劳无功。他是喜欢巴黎的，也喜欢路易。可人不能照着别人的方式去做自己的事；自私也好，不自私也罢，这是实话。况且，他也用不上。他尽可放心把莉莲托给路易照料，而谁也讨不到她更多的欢心，除了这个女婿。女婿（Beaux-fils），看来是老天安排好了的：做丈夫的不再是情人，便由女婿来顶丈夫的缺。莉莲所爱的那一百种傻气的小殷勤，马塞勒斯从不曾忘掉一样。最难得的是，他狂热地钦慕她，她那一份卓尔不群，在他眼里是无价之宝。钦慕她的人很多，可路易钦慕得比谁都深。这几年在莉莲身上强烈滋长起来的那一种世故、那一种想从场合和人身上榨取最大好处的意愿，在路易看来是自然又得宜的——正如在戈弗雷看来是不自然的一样。这一种世故本是莉莲身上一直就有的东西，只要它只表现为挑剔，还不是达到目的的手段，圣彼得从前也是喜欢它的。他知道，正因为这一份世故——更甚于妻子自己有一点私房钱这一事实——她和女儿们才从来不像某些教员太太那样灰扑扑、有些可怜相。她们没有多少，可从不寒碜。她们从不作寒碜的妥协。弄不到合适的，宁可不弄。通常她们总有合适的，至于怎么付的账，总归有法子。他说不上她们奢华；那栋旧屋是够滑稽、够空荡的了，可里面没有一件丑东西。
自打罗莎蒙德嫁给马塞勒斯，她和母亲两个人在某些方面都变得叫人迷惑——变了，也硬了。可路易——这个一手造成这一切的人——自己却毫发无损。凡事都去求他——为奥古斯塔，为克兰教授，为那些不及他们幸运的人受了伤的心情。他要谢绝这份王侯般的邀请，倒最不是因为路易。
他能从这件事里抽身而不伤任何人的心——虽说路易会难过，他心里明白。他只消一口咬定他得工作，说他离不开那间旧书房。做一个写史书的人，也有这点好处。书桌是一座可以躲在其后的掩体，是一个可以爬进去藏身的洞穴。

---

===Original===
When St. Peter told his family of his decision, Louie was disappointed; but he was respectful, and readily conceded that the Professor’s first duty was to his work. Rosamond was incredulous and piqued; she didn’t see how he could be so ungenerous as to spoil an arrangement which would give pleasure to everyone concerned. His wife looked at him with thoughtful disbelief.
When they were alone together, she approached the matter more directly than was her wont nowadays.
“Godfrey,” she said slowly and sadly, “I wonder what it is that makes you draw away from your family. Or who it is.”
“My dear, are you going to be jealous?”

===Chinese===
圣彼得把决定告诉家人的时候，路易很失望；可他是恭敬的，欣然承认教授的首要职责在他的工作。罗莎蒙德又难以置信、又气恼；她不明白他怎会这样小气，破坏一个能给所有相关人员都带来快乐的安排。他妻子带着若有所思的不相信神色望着他。
等到两人独处的时候，她处理这事比近来平常的做法更直截了当。
「戈弗雷，」她缓缓地、悲伤地说，「我纳闷，是什么让你从家里人身边抽身退去。或者是——谁。」
「亲爱的，你该不是要吃醋吧？」

===Original===
“I wish I were going to be. I’d much rather see you foolish about some woman than becoming lonely and inhuman.”
“Well, the habit of living with ideas grows on one, I suppose, just as inevitably as the more cheerful habit of living with various ladies. There’s something to be said for both.”
“I think your ideas were best when you were your most human self.”
St. Peter sighed. “I can’t contradict you there. But I must go on as I can. It is not always May.”

===Chinese===
「我倒宁愿我会。我宁可看你为了某个女人犯傻，也不愿你变得孤僻，不像个人。」
「嗯，我想，和念头住在一起的习惯，也和人同各种女士住在一起那种更愉快的习惯一样，是会不知不觉地缠上一个人的。两种习惯都各有各的好处。」
「我觉得你的念头，是在你最有人的气息的时候最好。」
圣彼得叹了口气。「这一点我没法反驳你。可我总得尽力走下去。日子不会永远是五月。」

===Original===
“You are not old enough for the pose you take. That’s what puzzles me. For so many years you never seemed to grow at all older, though I did. Two years ago you were an impetuous young man. Now you save yourself in everything. You’re naturally warm and affectionate; all at once you begin shutting yourself away from everybody. I don’t think you’ll be happier for it.” Up to this point she had been lecturing him. Now she suddenly crossed the room and sat down on the arm of his chair, looking into his face and twisting up the ends of his military eyebrows with her thumb and middle finger. “Why is it, Godfrey? I can’t see any change in your face, though I watch you so closely. It’s in your mind, in your mood. Something has come over you. Is it merely that you know too much, I wonder? Too much to be happy? You were always the wisest person in the world. What is it, can’t you tell me?”
“I can’t altogether tell myself, Lillian. It’s not wholly a matter of the calendar. It’s the feeling that I’ve put a great deal behind me, where I can’t go back to it again—and I don’t really wish to go back. The way would be too long and too fatiguing. Perhaps, for a home-staying man, I’ve lived pretty hard. I wasn’t willing to slight anything—you, or my desk, or my students. And now I seem to be tremendously tired. One pays, coming or going. A man has got only just so much in him; when it’s gone he slumps. Even the first Napoleon did.” They both laughed. That was an old joke—the Professor’s darkest secret. At the font he had been christened Napoleon Godfrey St. Peter. There had always been a Napoleon in the family, since a remote grandfather got his discharge from the Grande Armée. Godfrey had abbreviated his name in Kansas, and even his daughters didn’t know what it had been originally.

===Chinese===
「你还没有老到能摆出这副姿态的地步。这正是叫我困惑的地方。这么多年，你仿佛从未老过一点，虽说我是一直老了下去的。两年前你还是个血气方刚的年轻人。如今你事事都在省着自己。你天生是热乎的、有情的；忽然之间，你开始把自己从所有人身边关起来。我不觉得你会因此更快乐些。」说到这里为止，她都在训他。忽然，她穿过屋子走过来，坐在他椅子的扶手上，望着他的脸，用拇指和中指把他那两撇军人似的眉毛的眉梢往上捻。「到底是为什么，戈弗雷？我这么近地看着你，却看不出你脸上有什么变化。是在你的心里，在你的情绪里。有什么东西落到了你身上。我只纳闷，是不是仅仅因为你懂得太多了？多到没法快活？你向来是世上最聪明的人。到底是什么，你不能告诉我么？」
「我自己也说不全，莉莲。这不全是日历上的事。这是一种感觉——我已经把许多东西抛在身后了，抛到我再回不去的地方——而我也实在不想回去。那条路太长，也太累人。也许，对一个居家的人来说，我活得是够凶的了。我什么也不肯草率——你也好，我的书桌也好，我的学生也好。如今我好像累得要命。来去都要付代价。一个人身上的东西就只有那么多；用尽了，人就垮下去。连第一位拿破仑（Napoleon）也不例外。」两人都笑了。那是个老笑话——教授最深的秘密。在洗礼的圣水池前，他受洗的名字原是拿破仑·戈弗雷·圣彼得。自从一位远祖从大军（Grande Armée）里退役回家，这家就一直有个拿破仑。戈弗雷是在堪萨斯时把自己的名字缩简了的，连他的女儿们也不知道那名字原本是什么。

===Original===
“I think, you know,” he told his wife as he rose to go to bed, “that I’ll get my second wind. But for the present I don’t want anything very stimulating. Paris is too beautiful, and too full of memories.”

===Chinese===
「我想，」他起身去睡时对妻子说，「我会缓过第二口气的。只是眼下，我不要任何太刺激的东西。巴黎太美了，也太满都是回忆了。」

### XVI / 第十六

===Original===
One Saturday morning in the spring, when the Professor was at work in the old house, he heard energetic footsteps running up the uncarpeted stairway. Louie’s voice called:
“*Cher Papa*, shall I disturb you too much?”
St. Peter rose and opened to him. Louie was wearing his golf stockings, and a purple jacket with a fur collar.
“No, I’m not going golfing. I changed my mind, but didn’t have time to change my clothes. I want you to take a run out along the lake-shore with us. Rosie is going to lunch with some friends at the Country Club. We’ll have a drive with her, and then drop her there. It’s a glorious day.” Louie’s keen, interested eye ran about the shabby little room. He chuckled. “The old bear, he just likes his old den, doesn’t he? I can readily understand. Your children were born here. Not your daughters—your sons, your splendid Spanish-adventurer sons! I’m proud to be related to them, even by marriage. And your blanket, surely that’s a Spanish touch!” Louie pounced upon the purple blanket, threw it across his chest, and, moving aside the wire lady, studied himself in Augusta’s glass. “And a very proper dressing-gown it would make for Louie, wouldn’t it?”
“It was Outland’s—a precious possession. His lost chum brought it up from Mexico.”
“Was it Outland’s, indeed?” Louie stroked it and regarded it in the glass with increased admiration. “I can never forgive destiny that I hadn’t the chance to know that splendid fellow.”

===Chinese===
春天一个星期六的早晨，教授正在旧屋里伏案，忽然听见一阵虎虎的脚步跑上未铺地毯的楼梯。路易的声音喊道：
「*cher Papa*（亲爱的岳父），我打扰您太多了吧？」
圣彼得起身给他开门。路易穿着他的高尔夫长袜，上身着一件紫色、带毛皮领的外套。
「不，我不去打高尔夫。我改了主意，只是没来得及换衣裳。我想请您跟我们一块儿沿湖滨兜一趟风。罗西要和几个朋友在乡村俱乐部（Country Club）吃午饭。我们先陪她兜一圈，再把她送到那儿。今儿这天儿可真好。」路易那双敏锐、好奇的眼睛把这间寒酸的小屋四处打量了一遍。他轻笑起来。「这只老熊，他就是喜欢他这间老窝，是不是？这我太能理解了。您的孩子就是在这儿出生的。不是您的女儿们——是您的儿子们，您那些了不起的西班牙探险家儿子！能和他们沾上亲，哪怕只是姻亲，我都觉得骄傲。还有您这条毯子，这可真有点西班牙味儿！」路易扑向那条紫色毯子，把它披在胸前，把那位铁丝淑女挪开，对着奥古斯塔的镜子端详起自己来。「这给路易做晨衣倒挺合适，是不是？」
「这是奥特兰的东西——一件宝贝。他那位已故的伙伴从墨西哥带回来的。」
「真是奥特兰的？」路易抚摸着它，对着镜子愈加钦慕地端详着。「命运没让我有机会结识那位出色的人物，这事我永远不能原谅它。」

===Original===
The Professor’s eyebrows rose in puzzled interrogation. “It might have been awkward—about Rosie, you know.”
“I never think of him as a rival,” said Louie, throwing back the blanket with a wide gesture. “I think of him as a brother, an adored and gifted brother.”
Half an hour later they were spinning along through the country, just coming green, Rosamond and her father on the back seat, Louie facing them. It struck the Professor that Louie had something on his mind; his restless bright eyes watched his wife narrowly, as if to seize an opportune moment.
“You know, Doctor,” he said presently, “we’ve decided to give up our house before we go abroad, and cut off the rent. We’ll move the books and pictures up to Outland (and our wedding presents, of course), and the silver we’ll put in the bank. There won’t be much of our present furniture that we’ll need. I wonder if you could use any of it? And it has just occurred to me, Rosie,” here he leaned forward and tapped her knee, “that we might ask Scott and Kathleen to come round and select anything they like. No use bothering to sell it, we’d get so little.”

===Chinese===
教授的眉毛困惑地扬起，像在追问。「那——关于罗西，您知道——说不定会有些尴尬呢。」
「我从不把他当作情敌，」路易说，一个夸张的大动作把毯子甩了回去。「我把他当作兄弟，一个我所爱慕的、有才华的兄弟。」
半小时后，他们已驱车飞驰在乡间，万物正刚刚返青。罗莎蒙德和父亲坐在后座，路易面对着他们。教授忽然觉得路易心里有事；他那双不安分的、明亮的眼睛紧紧盯着妻子，仿佛在伺机而动。
「您知道吗，博士，」过了一会儿他说，「我们决定出国前把这边的房子退了，把租金也省了。我们把书和画都搬到奥特兰去（当然，还有我们收到的结婚礼物），银器存进银行。眼下这些家具，我们用得着的没有多少。不知您能不能用得上其中一些？还有，我刚想到，罗西，」说到这里他探过身去，拍了拍她的膝盖，「我们不妨请司各特和凯瑟琳过来，挑些他们喜欢的东西。费劲去卖也犯不上，卖不了几个钱。」

===Original===
Rosamond looked at him in astonishment. It was very evident they had not discussed anything of this sort before. “Don’t be foolish, Louie,” she said quietly. “They wouldn’t want your things.”
“But why not?” he persisted playfully. “They are very nice things. Not right for Outland, but perfectly right for a little house. We chose them with care, and we don’t want them going into some dirty secondhand shop.”
“They won’t have to. We can store them in the attic at Outland, Heaven knows it’s big enough! You don’t have to do anything with them just now.”
“It seems a pity, when somebody might be getting the good of them. I know Scott could do very well with that chiffonier of mine. He admired it greatly, I remember, and said he’d never had one with proper drawers for his shirts.”
Rosamond’s lip curled.

===Chinese===
罗莎蒙德惊愕地望着他。显而易见，他们从前没商量过任何这样的事。「别犯傻了，路易，」她平静地说。「他们不会要你的东西的。」
「可为什么不要呢？」他嬉皮笑脸地追问。「都是些很好的东西。放在奥特兰不合适，放在一栋小房子里却再合适不过。当初我们都是精心挑选的，总不愿它们流进什么脏兮兮的旧货铺子吧。」
「它们用不着流到那种地方。我们可以把它们存到奥特兰的阁楼里，老天知道那地方够大的！你这会儿根本不必拿它们怎么样。」
「可要是有人能得着它们的益处，那多可惜。我知道司各特用我那只五斗橱一定挺合适。我记得他当年十分欣赏它，还说他从来没用过一只抽屉像样、能好好放衬衫的橱子呢。」
罗莎蒙德的嘴唇撇了撇。

===Original===
“Don’t look like that, Rosie! It’s naughty. Stop it!” Louie reached forward and shook her gently by the elbows. “And how can you be sure the McGregors wouldn’t like our things, when you’ve never asked them? What positive ideas she does get into her head!”
“They wouldn’t want them because they are ours, yours and mine, if you will have it,” she said coldly, drawing away from him.
Louie sank back into his seat and gave it up. “Why do you think such naughty things? I don’t believe it, you know! You are so touchy. Scott and Kitty may be a little standoffish, but it might very possibly make them feel better if you went at them nicely about this.” He rallied and began to coax again. “She’s got it into her head that the McGregors have a grudge, Doctor. There’s nothing to it.”

===Chinese===
「别这样，罗西！这可不好。别这样！」路易探过身，轻轻摇着她的两肘。「再说，你又没问过他们，怎么能这么肯定麦格雷戈一家不喜欢我们的东西呢？她脑子里竟装着这些莫须有的念头！」
「他们不会要的，就因为这些东西是我们的，你的和我的，非要说穿的话，」她冷冷地说，从他身边挪开。
路易倒回座位上，放弃的样子。「你怎么尽想这些不好的念头呢？我才不信呢，你知道的！你这人太敏感。司各特和基蒂也许是有点冷淡，可要是你好好地跟他们提这件事，说不定倒会让他们心里舒坦些。」他又打起精神，重新哄劝起来。「博士，她认定麦格雷戈一家怀恨在心呢。其实没那回事。」

===Original===
Rosamond had grown quite pale. Her upper lip, that was so like her mother’s when she was affable, so much harder when she was not, came down like a steel curtain. “I happen to know, Louie, that Scott blackballed you for the Arts and Letters. You can call that a grudge or not, as you please.”
Marsellus was visibly shaken. He looked sad. “Well, if he did, it wasn’t very nice of him, certainly. But are you sure, Rosie? Rumours do go about, and people like to stir up family differences.”
“It isn’t people, and it’s not rumour. I know it positively. Kathleen’s best friend told me.”
Louie lay back and shook with laughter. “Oh, the ladies, the ladies! What they do to each other, Professor!”

===Chinese===
罗莎蒙德的脸已变得十分苍白。她的上唇——和气时和她母亲如出一辙、不高兴时便硬得多——此刻像一道钢幕落了下来。「路易，我恰好知道，司各特在文学艺术会的入会投票上投了你的黑球。这事你爱叫它怀恨也好，不叫也好，随你。」
马塞勒斯显然受了震动。他看上去很难过。「唉，要是他真那么做了，那当然不太好。可是你拿得准吗，罗西？风言风语总是有的，人也爱挑拨人家家里的是非。」
「不是旁人，也不是风言风语。我千真万确地知道。凯瑟琳最要好的朋友亲口告诉我的。」
路易往后一仰，笑得直颤。「哎，这些太太们，这些太太们！教授，她们彼此之间下得去的手啊！」

===Original===
St. Peter was very uncomfortable. “I don’t think I’d accept such evidence, Rosamond. I don’t believe it of Scott, and I think Louie has the right idea. People are like children, and Scott’s poor and proud. I think Louie’s chiffonier would go to his heart, if Louie offered it to him. I’m afraid you wouldn’t do it very graciously.”
“Professor, I’ll go to McGregor’s office and put it up to him. If he scorns it, so much the worse for him. He’ll lose a very handy piece of furniture.”
Rosamond’s paleness changed to red. Fortunately they were spinning over the gravel loops that led through shaven turf to the Country Club. “You can do as you like with your own things, Louie. But I don’t want any of mine in the McGregors’ bungalow. I know Scott’s brand of humour too well, and the kind of jokes that would be made about them.”

===Chinese===
圣彼得十分不安。「这种证据我恐怕不能采信，罗莎蒙德。我不相信司各特会做出这种事，我觉得路易的想法是对的。人都跟孩子似的，而司各特又穷又要面子。我想路易那只五斗橱，要是由路易送给他，准会叫他感动的。我只怕你去办这件事时，不会办得太好看就是了。」
「博士，我亲自到麦格雷戈的办公室去，把这事当面跟他提。他要是瞧不上，那是他倒霉。他将失去一件很派用场的家具。」
罗莎蒙德脸上的苍白转成了潮红。幸好这时车子已驶上那一条条在修剪齐整的草坪间蜿蜒、通往乡村俱乐部的碎石环形道。「你自己的东西，随你怎么处置，路易。可我不想把我任何一件东西送进麦格雷戈家那栋小平房。司各特那一路的幽默我是太领教了，我也太清楚这些东西到了他们嘴里会被编成什么样的笑话。」

===Original===
The car stopped. Louie sprang out and gave his arm to his wife. He walked up the steps to the door with her, and his back expressed such patient, protecting kindness that the Professor bit his lower lip with indignation. Louie came back looking quite grey and tired, and sank into the seat beside the Professor with a sadder-and-wiser smile.
“Louie,” St. Peter spoke with deep feeling, “do you happen to have read a novel of Henry James, *The American*? There’s a rather nice scene in it, in which a young Frenchman, hurt in a duel, apologizes for the behaviour of his family. I’d like to do something of the sort. I apologize to you for Rosamond, and for Scott, if he has done such a mean thing.”

===Chinese===
车子停了下来。路易跳下车，把胳膊伸给妻子。他陪她走上台阶，向大门走去，他的背影透出那样一种耐心的、护佑般的体贴，惹得教授气愤地咬住了下唇。路易回来时面色灰白、神色疲倦，在教授身边的座位上颓然坐下，嘴角挂着一丝吃了亏、长了智的笑。
「路易，」圣彼得动情地说，「你读过亨利·詹姆斯（Henry James）的一本小说《美国人》（*The American*）吗？里面有一段挺精彩的情节：一个年轻的法国人决斗受了伤，却替他家人的行为道歉。我倒很想学一学他的样子。我替罗莎蒙德向你道歉，也替司各特——如果他果真做出了这种卑鄙的事。」

===Original===
Louie’s downcast face brightened at once. He squeezed the Professor’s arm warmly. “Oh, *that’s* all right, sir! As for Scott, I can understand. He was the first son of the family, and he was the whole thing. Then I came along, a stranger, and carried off Rosie, and this patent began to pay so well—it’s enough to make any man jealous, and he a Scotchman! But I think Scott will come around in the end; people usually do, if you treat them well, and I mean to. I like the fellow. As for Rosamond, you mustn’t give that a thought. I love her when she’s naughty. She’s a bit unreasonable sometimes, but I’m always hoping for a period of utter, of fantastic unreasonableness, which will be the beginning of a great happiness for us all.”
“Louie, you are magnanimous and magnificent!” murmured his vanquished father-in-law.

===Chinese===
路易低垂的脸登时亮了起来。他热切地紧了紧教授的胳膊。「哎呀，*那*（*that’s*）没什么，先生！至于司各特，我能体谅。他是这家的长子，从前是一家的全部。后来我来了，一个外人，把罗西拐走了，而这项专利又开始这样赚钱——换作任何人都够叫他嫉妒的了，何况他还是个苏格兰人！不过我想司各特终究会回心转意的；人一般都会，只要你好好待他们，我也打算这么待他。我喜欢这小子。至于罗莎蒙德，您千万别往心里去。她犯起小性儿来，我倒爱她爱得更深。她有时是有点不讲道理，可我一直盼着有那么一段彻底的、近乎荒诞的不讲道理的时期，那将是我们所有人巨大幸福的开端。」
「路易，你真是宽宏大量，了不起！」他那被打败的岳父低声喃喃道。

### XVII / XVII

===Original===
Lillian and the Marselluses sailed for France early in May. The Professor, left alone, had plenty of time to spray his rose-vines, and his garden had never been so beautiful as it was that June. After his university duties were over, he smuggled his bed and clothing back to the old house and settled down to a leisurely bachelor life. He realized that he ought to be getting to work. The garden, in which he sat all day, was no longer a valid excuse to keep him from his study. But the task that awaited him up there was difficult. It was a little thing, but one of those little things at which the hand becomes self-conscious, feels itself stiff and clumsy.

===Chinese===
五月初，莉莲和马塞勒斯一家坐船去了法国。教授落了单，倒有的是工夫给他的蔷薇藤喷药水，而他的花园也从来没有像那年六月那般美丽过。大学里的课务一了结，他便悄悄把床和衣物搬回了旧屋，安顿下来，过起从容的单身日子。他心里明白，自己该着手干活了。那座他整日坐在其中的花园，已不能再作为把他挡在书房之外的理由。可是在楼上等着他的那桩活计，却不好对付。事情不大，却正是那种小事——一着手，手就变得不自在起来，觉得自己又僵又笨。

===Original===
It was his plan to give part of this summer to Tom Outland’s diary﻿—to edit and annotate it for publication. The bother was that he must write an introduction. The diary covered only about six months of the boy’s life, a summer he spent on the Blue Mesa, and in it there was almost nothing about Tom himself. To mean anything, it must be prefaced by a sketch of Outland, and some account of his later life and achievements. To write of his scientific work would be comparatively easy. But that was not all the story; his was a many-sided mind, though a simple and straightforward personality.

===Chinese===
他打算把这个夏天的一部分，留给汤姆·奥特兰的日记——为它做编辑、加注，准备出版。麻烦的是，他得写一篇引言。那部日记只记了这个孩子大约半年的生活，是他在蓝色台地（the Blue Mesa）度过的那个夏天，里面几乎没有一句关于汤姆自己的话。要想有一点意义，前面就得加上奥特兰的一篇小传，再讲讲他后来的生活和成就。写他的科学工作，倒还比较容易。可那并不是全部；他的头脑是多面的，尽管他这个人单纯而坦率。

===Original===
Of course Mrs. St. Peter had insisted that he was not altogether straightforward; but that was merely because he was not altogether consistent. As an investigator he was clear-sighted and hardheaded; but in personal relations he was apt to be exaggerated and quixotic. He idealized the people he loved and paid his devoir to the ideal rather than to the individual, so that his behaviour was sometimes a little too exalted for the circumstances﻿—“chivalry of the cinema,” Lillian used to say. One of his sentimental superstitions was that he must never on any account owe any material advantage to his friends, that he must keep affection and advancement far apart, as if they were chemicals that would disintegrate each other. St. Peter thought this the logical result of Tom’s strange bringing-up and his early associations. There is, he knew, this dream of self-sacrificing friendship and disinterested love down among the day-labourers, the men who run the railroad trains and boats and reapers and thrashers and mine-drills of the world. And Tom had brought it along to the university, where advancement through personal influence was considered honourable.

===Chinese===
当然，圣彼得太太一口咬定他并不全然坦率；可那不过是因为他并不前后一致罢了。作为一个研究的人，他目光清明、头脑冷静；可是在人与人的关系上，他却往往夸张、堂吉诃德式。他把他所爱的人理想化，他致敬的是那个理想、而非那个具体的人，所以他的举止有时对所处境况而言未免太高了些——「银幕式的骑士精神」，莉莲过去常这么说。他有一种感伤的迷信：无论如何不能亏欠朋友任何物质上的好处，必须把感情和进身之阶远远隔开，仿佛它们是两种一经接触便会彼此分解的化学药品。圣彼得认为，这是汤姆那古怪的教养和他早年的交游合乎逻辑的结果。他知道，在那些做日工的人当中，在那些开火车、开船、开收割机、打谷机、矿井钻机的人们当中，是有着这样一种自我牺牲的友谊、这样一种无私的爱的梦想的。而汤姆把这种梦想也带到了大学里——在那儿，靠私人提携而进身，是被视为体面的。

===Original===
It was not until Outland was a senior that Lillian began to be jealous of him. He had been almost a member of the family for two years, and she had never found fault with the boy. But after the Professor began to take Tom up to the study and talk over his work with him, began to make a companion of him, then Mrs. St. Peter withdrew her favour. She could change like that; friendship was not a matter of habit with her. And when she was through with anyone, she of course found reasons for her fickleness. Tom, she reminded her husband, was far from frank, though he had such an open manner. He had been consistently reserved about his own affairs, and she could not believe the facts he withheld were altogether creditable. They had always known he had a secret, something to do with the mysterious Rodney Blake and the bank account in New Mexico upon which he was not at liberty to draw. The young man must have felt the change in her, for he began that winter to make his work a pretext for coming to the house less often. He and St. Peter now met in the alcove behind the Professor’s lecture room at the university.

===Chinese===
直到奥特兰升上四年级，莉莲才开始嫉妒他。有两年的工夫，他几乎算是这个家里的一员，她从没挑过这孩子的错处。可自从教授开始把汤姆带上书房，和他谈他的功课，开始把他当作一个同伴，圣彼得太太便收回了自己的青眼。她是说变就变的；友谊在她不是一件出于习惯的事。而一旦她与谁断了，她自然总会为自己的反复找到理由。她提醒丈夫，汤姆虽说举止那样坦荡，实则远非坦率。他一直对自己的事守口如瓶，她不信他所瞒着不说的事桩桩件件都拿得出手。他们早就知道他有一桩秘密，与那个神秘的罗德尼·布雷克（Rodney Blake）、与新墨西哥那笔他无权支取的银行存款有关。这年轻人想必是察觉了她的变化，因为那年冬天起，他便拿功课作借口，不大上这宅子里来了。他和圣彼得如今改在大学里教授讲堂后面的那间凹室中见面。

===Original===
One Sunday, shortly before Tom’s Commencement, he came to the house to ask Rosamond to go to the senior dance with him. The family were having tea in the garden; a few days of intensely warm weather had come on and hurried the roses into bloom. Rosamond happened to ask Tom, who sat in his white flannels, fanning himself with his straw hat, if spring in the Southwest was as warm as this.

===Chinese===
汤姆毕业典礼前不久的一个星期天，他来到这宅子里，请罗莎蒙德去参加毕业舞会。一家人正在花园里喝茶；连着几天酷热的天气，催得玫瑰一齐开了。罗莎蒙德顺口问汤姆——他穿着一身白色法兰绒，正拿草帽扇着风——西南部的春天，是不是也像这般热。

===Original===
“Oh, no,” he replied. “May is usually chilly down there﻿—bright sun, but a kind of edge in the wind, and cool nights. Last night reminded me of smothery May nights in Washington.”

Mrs. St. Peter glanced up. “You mean Washington City? I didn’t know you had ever been so far east.”

There was no denying that the young man looked uncomfortable. He frowned and said in a low voice: “Yes, I’ve been there. I suppose I don’t speak of it because I haven’t very pleasant recollections of it.”

“How long were you there?” his hostess asked.

“A winter and spring, more than six months. Long enough to get very homesick.” He went away almost at once, as if he were afraid of being questioned further.

===Chinese===
「哦，不，」他答道，「五月在那边通常还是凉的——太阳明晃晃的，可风里有一种锋刃似的东西，夜里也凉。昨晚就让我想起华盛顿那种闷得人喘不过气来的五月之夜。」

圣彼得太太抬眼瞥了一下。「你说的是华盛顿城？我可不知道你还到过那么东边。」

这年轻人面有窘色，那是没法否认的。他皱起眉头，低声说：「是的，我去过那儿。我想我不提它，是因为我对它没有什么愉快的回忆。」

「你在那儿待了多久？」女主人问。

「一个冬天一个春天，六个多月。够叫人想家想到极点了。」他几乎马上就走开了，仿佛生怕再被追问下去。

===Original===
The subject came up again a few weeks later, however. After Tom’s graduation, two courses were open to him. He was offered an instructorship, with a small salary, in the Physics department under Dr. Crane, and a graduate scholarship at Johns Hopkins University. St. Peter strongly urged him to accept the latter. One evening when the family were discussing Tom’s prospects, the Professor summed up all the reasons why he ought to go to Baltimore and work in the laboratory made famous by Dr. Rowland. He assured him, moreover, that he would find the atmosphere of an old Southern city delightful.

“Yes, I know something about the atmosphere,” Tom broke out at last. “It is delightful, but it’s all wrong for me. It discourages me dreadfully. I used to go over there when I was in Washington, and it always made me blue. I don’t believe I could ever work there.”

“But can you trust a child’s impressions to guide you now, in such an important decision?” asked Mrs. St. Peter gravely.

“I wasn’t a child, Mrs. St. Peter. I was as much grown up as I am now﻿—older, in some ways. It was only about a year before I came here.”

“But, Tom, you were on the section gang that year! Why do you mix us all up?” Kathleen caught his hand and squeezed the knuckles together, as she did when she wanted to punish him.

“Well, maybe it was two years before. It doesn’t matter. It was long enough to count for two ordinary years,” he muttered abstractedly.

Again he went away abruptly, and a few days later he told St. Peter that he had definitely accepted the instructorship under Crane, and would stay on in Hamilton.

===Chinese===
然而几个星期之后，这话题又被提了起来。汤姆毕业之后，面前摆着两条路。一是克兰博士名下的物理系给他一个助教职位，薪金微薄；二是约翰斯·霍普金斯大学给他一份研究生奖学金。圣彼得力劝他接受后者。一天傍晚，一家人正讨论汤姆的前程，教授把该去巴尔的摩、进罗兰博士那间出了名的实验室工作的种种理由一一摆了出来。他还向他保证，一座南方古城的氛围，他一定会觉得可爱。

「是的，那氛围我略知一二，」汤姆终于脱口而出，「它是可爱，可对我全不合适。它叫我灰心极了。我在华盛顿那会儿常到那边去，每去一回就心情低落一回。我不相信我能在那儿工作。」

「可是，汤姆，在这么要紧的决定上，你能信一个孩子的印象来替你拿主意吗？」圣彼得太太郑重地问。

「我不是孩子了，圣彼得太太。那时我和现在一样是个大人——在某些方面，比现在还老成些。那也不过是我来这儿之前大约一年的事。」

「可是，汤姆，那年你还在铁路工班上干活呢！你怎么把我们全搅糊涂了？」凯瑟琳一把抓住他的手，把他的指节捏在一起——她要罚他的时候就是这样。

「呃，也许是来这儿之前两年的事。那不要紧。反正够长的，抵得上寻常的两年了，」他心不在焉地咕哝道。

他又一次突然走开了。几天之后，他告诉圣彼得，他已正式接受了克兰名下的助教职位，要留在汉密尔顿了。

===Original===
During that summer after Outland’s graduation, St. Peter got to know all there was behind his reserve. Mrs. St. Peter and the two girls were in Colorado, and the Professor was alone in the house, writing on volumes three and four of his history. Tom was carrying on some experiments of his own, over in the Physics laboratory. He and St. Peter were often together in the evening, and on fine afternoons they went swimming. Every Saturday the Professor turned his house over to the cleaning-woman, and he and Tom went to the lake and spent the day in his sailboat.

It was just the sort of summer St. Peter liked, if he had to be in Hamilton at all. He was his own cook, and had laid in a choice assortment of cheeses and light Italian wines from a discriminating importer in Chicago. Every morning before he sat down at his desk he took a walk to the market and had his pick of the fruits and salads. He dined at eight o’clock. When he cooked a fine leg of lamb, saignant, well rubbed with garlic before it went into the pan, then he asked Outland to dinner. Over a dish of steaming asparagus, swathed in a napkin to keep it hot, and a bottle of sparkling Asti, they talked and watched night fall in the garden. If the evening happened to be rainy or chilly, they sat inside and read Lucretius.

It was on one of those rainy nights, before the fire in the dining-room, that Tom at last told the story he had always kept back. It was nothing very incriminating, nothing very remarkable; a story of youthful defeat, the sort of thing a boy is sensitive about﻿—until he grows older.

===Chinese===
奥特兰毕业之后的那个夏天，圣彼得终于弄明白了他那缄默背后的一切。圣彼得太太和两个女儿在科罗拉多，教授独自一人守着这宅子，撰写他史书的第三、第四卷。汤姆则在物理实验室里做着他自己的一些实验。傍晚，他和圣彼得常在一处；晴朗的午后，他们便去游泳。每个星期六，教授把宅子让给清扫女工，他和汤姆便到湖上去，在他的帆船上消磨一整天。

这正是圣彼得所喜欢的那种夏天——如果他非待在汉密尔顿不可的话。他自己掌厨，还从芝加哥一个有品位的进口商那里，备办了一批精选的奶酪和清淡的意大利葡萄酒。每天早上，他在书桌前坐下之前，都要散步到市场去，亲手挑选水果和沙拉用的菜蔬。他八点钟吃晚饭。每逢他烤一只上好的羊腿，烤得带血（*saignant*），下锅前用大蒜细细擦过——他便请奥特兰来吃饭。就着一盘热气腾腾的芦笋（用一条餐巾裹住保温）和一瓶阿斯蒂（Asti）气泡酒，他们一边谈天，一边看夜色在花园里降临。倘若那晚碰上落雨或转凉，他们便坐在屋里，读卢克莱修（Lucretius）。

就是在那样一个雨夜，在餐室的炉火前，汤姆终于讲出了那个他一直藏着的故事。那并不是什么足以定罪的勾当，也没什么了不起；不过是一桩少年时的败绩，是那种男孩子会为之敏感的事——直到他长大一些。

### I / I

===Original===
The thing that sidetracked me and made me so late coming to college was a somewhat unusual accident, or string of accidents. It began with a poker game, when I was a call boy in Pardee, New Mexico.
One cold, clear night in the fall I started out to hunt up a freight crew that was to go out soon after midnight. It was just after pay day, and one of the fellows had tipped me off that there would be a poker game going on in the cardroom behind the Ruby Light saloon. I knew most of my crew would be there, except Conductor Willis, who had a sick baby at home. The front windows were dark, of course. I went up the back alley, through a tumbledown ice house and a court, into a ’dobe room that didn’t open into the saloon proper at all. It was crowded, and hot and stuffy enough. There were six or seven in the game, and a crowd of fellows were standing about the walls, rubbing the whitewash off on to their coat shoulders. There was a birdcage hanging in one window, covered with an old flannel shirt, but the canary had woken up and was singing away for dear life. He was a beautiful singer—an old Mexican had trained him—and he was one of the attractions of the place.

===Chinese===
那桩把我岔开、害我这么晚才上大学的事，是一次不大寻常的意外——或者说，一连串意外。事情起于一场扑克牌局，那时我在新墨西哥（New Mexico）的帕迪（Pardee）当叫班工。
秋天一个清冷晴朗的夜里，我出门去找一伙过了午夜不久就要出车的货运班组。刚发过薪水，有个伙计给我透了底，说「红宝石之光」酒馆后面那间牌室里准有一场牌局。我们那班组的人多半会在那儿，只除了列车长威利斯（Willis）——他家有个吃奶的孩子病着。前面的窗户当然是黑的。我顺着后巷走，穿过一座快塌的冰库和一个院子，进了一间土坯房（’dobe room），那屋子根本不和酒馆正堂相通。里面挤得很，又闷又热。牌桌上有六七个人，一帮伙计贴墙站着，把墙上的石灰粉蹭到了各自的衣肩上。一扇窗上挂着一只鸟笼，拿一件旧法兰绒衬衫盖着，可那只金丝雀已经醒了，正拼命地唱着。它是个顶出色的歌手——一个老墨西哥人调教出来的——算得上这地方的一个招牌。

===Original===
I happened along when a jackpot was running. Two of the fellows I’d come for were in it, and they naturally wanted to finish the hand. I stood by the door with my watch, keeping time for them. Among the players I saw two sheep men who always liked a lively game, and one of the bystanders told me you had to buy a hundred dollars’ worth of chips to get in that night. The crowd was fussing about one fellow, Rodney Blake, who had come in from his engine without cleaning up. That wasn’t customary; the minute a man got in from his run, he took a bath, put on citizen’s clothes, and went to a barber. This Blake was a new fireman on our division. He’d come up town in his greasy overalls and sweaty blue shirt, with his face streaked up with smoke. He’d been drinking; he smelled of it, and his eyes were out of focus. All the other men were clean and freshly shaved, and they were sore at Blake—said his hands were so greasy they marked the cards. Some of them wanted to put him out of the game, but he was a big, heavy-built fellow, and nobody wanted to be the man to do it. It didn’t please them any better when he took the jackpot.

===Chinese===
我赶过去的时候，正赶上一个大彩池。我要找的两个人中有两个正在局里，自然想把这一手打完。我拿着表站在门边，替他们掐着点。牌桌当中，我认出了两个养羊的伙计，他们一向爱玩大的；旁边一个看客告诉我，那晚要上桌，非得买一百美元的筹码不可。那一屋子人正为一个家伙闹得不痛快——罗德尼·布雷克（Rodney Blake），他是刚从机车上下来的，连脸都没洗一把。这可不合规矩；一个人一跑完车，头一件事就是洗澡、换上便装、上理发店。这个布雷克是我们这一段新来的司炉工。他穿着一身油乎乎的工装裤、一件汗透的蓝衬衫就上了街，脸上还带着一道道烟灰的痕迹。他喝了酒；一身酒气，眼神也散了焦。在场的别人都干干净净、刚刮过脸，都对布雷克一肚子火——说他那双手油得在牌上留印子。有几个想把他赶出局，可他是个膀大腰圆的壮汉，谁也不想当那个出头的人。等他一把赢走了大彩池，大伙儿心里就更不痛快了。

===Original===
I got my two men and hurried them out, and two others from the row along the wall took their places. One of the chaps who left with me asked me to go up to his house and get his grip with his work clothes. He’d lost every cent of his pay cheque and didn’t want to face his wife. I asked him who was winning.
“Blake. The dirty boomer’s been taking everything. But the fellows will clean him out before morning.”

===Chinese===
我把那两个人带出来催着走了，靠墙站着的那排人里又有两个顶了他们的缺。跟我一块儿出来的一个小伙子求我去他家一趟，替他取那只装工作服的旅行袋。他把工资输了个精光，没脸见老婆。我问他谁在赢。
「布雷克。这不要脸的流浪汉什么都赢走了。不过大伙儿天亮前准能把他刮个干净。」

===Original===
About two o’clock, when my work for that night was over and I was going home to sleep, I just dropped in at the cardroom to see how things had come out. The game was breaking up. Since I left them at midnight, they had changed to stud poker, and Blake, the fireman, had cleaned everybody out. He was cashing in his chips when I came in. The bank was a little short, but Blake made no fuss about it. He had something over sixteen hundred dollars lying on the table before him in banknotes and gold. Some of the crowd were insulting him, trying to get him into a fight and loot him. He paid no attention and began to put the money away, not looking at anybody. The bills he folded and put inside the band of his hat. He filled his overalls pockets with the gold, and swept the rest of it into his big red neckerchief.
I’d been interested in this fellow ever since he came on our division; he was closemouthed and unfriendly. He was one of those fellows with a settled, mature body and a young face, such as you often see among workingmen. There was something calm, and sarcastic, and mocking about his expression—that, too, you often see among workingmen. When he had put all his money away, he got up and walked toward the door without a word, without saying good night to anybody.

===Chinese===
大约两点钟，我那夜的活儿干完了，正要回家睡觉，就顺脚拐进那间牌室看看结局如何。牌局正在散场。从午夜我离开他们那时起，他们改打起了梭哈，那个司炉工布雷克把所有人都刮了个干净。我进去时，他正在把筹码兑成现钱。庄上差了一点儿，布雷克倒也没计较。他面前桌上摆着一千六百多美元，都是钞票和金币。人群里有几个在骂他，想挑他打架、好趁火打劫。他一概不理，开始收拾钱，也不看任何人。钞票他折好塞进帽箍里。金币把工装裤口袋装得满满的，剩下的全扫进他那条大红围巾里。
自打他到了我们这一段，我就对这家伙起了兴趣；他是个闷葫芦，不大合群。他是那种身架沉稳老成、脸却年轻的人——干力气活的里头常能见到这路人。他的神情里有一种沉静的、讥讽的、嘲弄的东西——这个，你也常在干活的人脸上见到。他把钱全都收好了，站起身，一声不吭地朝门口走去，没跟任何人道一声晚安。

===Original===
“Manners of a hog, and a dirty hog!” little Barney Shea yelled after him. Blake’s back was just in the doorway; he hitched up one shoulder, but didn’t turn or make a sound.
I slipped out after him and followed him down the street. His walk was unsteady, and the gold in his baggy overalls pockets clinked with every step he took. I ran a little way and caught up with him. “What are you going to do with all that money, Blake?” I asked him.
“Lose it, tomorrow night. I’m no hog for money. Damned barber-pole dudes!”

===Chinese===
「猪一样的教养，还是头脏猪！」矮个子巴尼·谢（Barney Shea）冲着他背影嚷。布雷克的背正卡在门框里；他耸了耸一边肩膀，却没回头，也没出声。
我悄悄跟着他出了门，顺街跟在他后头。他走路晃晃悠悠的，宽松的工装裤口袋里的金币每走一步就叮当响一下。我小跑了几步赶上他。「布雷克，这么多钱你打算怎么花？」我问他。
「输掉它，明儿晚上。我对钱可不贪心。这帮该死的剃须杆花架子阔少！」

===Original===
I thought I’d better follow him home. I knew he lodged with an old Mexican woman, in the yellow quarter, behind the roundhouse. His room opened on to the street, by a sky-blue door. He went in, didn’t strike a light or make a stab at undressing, but threw himself just as he was on the bed and went to sleep. His hat stuck between the iron rods of the bed-head, the gold ran out of his pockets and rolled over the bare floor in the dark.
I struck a match and lit a candle. The bed took up half the room; on the dresser was a grip with his clean clothes in it, just as he’d brought it in from his run. I took out the clothes and began picking up the money; got the bills out of his hat, emptied his pockets, and collected the coins that lay in the hollow of the bed about his hips, and put it all into the grip. Then I blew out the light and sat down to listen. I trusted all the boys who were at the Ruby Light that night, except Barney Shea. He might try to pull something off on a stranger, down in Mexican town. We had a quiet night, however, and a cold one. I found Blake’s winter overcoat hanging on the wall and wrapped up in it. I wasn’t a bit sorry when the roosters began to crow and the dogs began barking all over Mexican town. At last the sun came up and turned the desert and the ’dobe town red in a minute. I began to shake the man on the bed. Waking men who didn’t want to get up was part of my job, and I didn’t let up on him until I had him on his feet.

===Chinese===
我想我还是跟着他回家为好。我知道他寄住在一个老墨西哥女人家里，在机车库后面那个黄皮肤区。他那间屋的门朝着街，漆成天蓝色。他进了屋，既没点灯，也没动手脱衣，就那么原样一头栽倒在床上睡着了。他的帽子卡在床头的铁栏杆中间，金币从口袋里滚出来，在黑暗里满光地板地乱滚。
我划了一根火柴，点上一支蜡烛。那张床占了半间屋子；梳妆台上放着一只旅行袋，里头是他那身干净衣裳，还是他跑车回来时拎进来的样子。我把衣裳掏出来，开始捡钱；从帽子里把钞票取出来，掏空他的口袋，把在他腰边床褥凹处散落的硬币都收拢，全装进那只旅行袋里。然后我吹灭了灯，坐下来听动静。那夜在「红宝石之光」的人我都信得过，只除了巴尼·谢。在墨西哥人镇那边，他对一个外乡人说不定会动手脚。然而那一夜很太平，也很冷。我找到布雷克挂在墙上的冬大衣，把自己裹了进去。等到公鸡开始打鸣、满墨西哥人镇的狗都叫起来，我可一点儿也不觉得难受。太阳终于升起来，一眨眼就把那片沙漠和那座土坯小镇染成了红色。我开始摇床上那个人。把不想起床的人叫醒，本就是我分内的事，我不依不饶，直到把他从床上弄起来站直了为止。

===Original===
“Hello, kid, come to call me?”
I told him I’d come to call him to a Harvey House breakfast. “You owe me a good one. I brought you home last night.”
“Sure, I’m glad to have company. Wait till I wash up a bit.” He took his soap and towel and comb and went out into the patio, a neat little sanded square with flowers and vines all around, and washed at the trough under the pump. Then he called me to come and pump water on his head. After he’d stood the gush of cold water for a few seconds, he straightened up with his teeth chattering.
“That ought to get the whisky out of a fellow’s head, oughtn’t it? Felt good, Tom.” Presently he began feeling his side pockets. “Was I dreaming something, or did I take a string of jackpots last night?”
“The money’s in your grip,” I told him. “You don’t deserve it, for you were too drunk to take care of it. I had to come after you and pick it up out of the mud.”
“All right. I’ll go halvers. Easy come, easy go.”
I told him I didn’t want anything off him but breakfast, and I wanted that pretty soon.
“Go easy, son. I’ve got to change my shirt. This one’s wet.”
“It’s worse than wet. You oughtn’t to go up town without changing. You’re a stranger here, and it makes a bad impression.”

===Chinese===
「嗨，小子，来叫我班的？」
我跟他说，我来叫他，是请他去哈维之家（Harvey House）吃一顿早饭。「你欠我个人情。昨儿夜里是我把你弄回来的。」
「行啊，有伴儿我乐意。等我稍微洗一把。」他拿着肥皂、毛巾和梳子走进院子——那是个齐齐整整、铺着沙子的小方院，四周围满花和藤蔓——在水泵下面的石槽边洗起来。接着他喊我过去给他往头上抽水。他在那股凉水里淋了几秒钟，便直起腰来，牙齿捉对儿打架。
「这下子谁脑袋里的威士忌都该冲干净了，是不是？真痛快，汤姆。」过了一会儿他开始摸自己两侧的口袋。「我是做了个什么梦呢，还是昨儿夜里我连着赢了一串大彩池？」
「钱在你旅行袋里，」我告诉他。「这钱你不配得，你醉成那样根本顾不上它。我只好跟着你，从泥地里把它捡起来。」
「成。咱俩对半分。来得容易，去得也快。」
我跟他说，我不要他任何东西，只要一顿早饭，而且要快。
「悠着点儿，小子。我得换件衬衫。这件湿透了。」
「岂止是湿。你不该不换衣裳就上街。你在这儿是外乡人，这样给人印象不好。」

===Original===
He shrugged his shoulders and looked superior. He had a square-built, honest face and steady eyes that didn’t carry a cynical expression very well. I knew he was a decent chap, though he’d been drinking and acting ugly ever since he’d been on our division.
After breakfast we went out and sat in the sun at a place where the wooden sidewalk ran over a sand gully and made a sort of bridge. I had a long talk with him. I was carrying the grip with his winnings in it, and I finally persuaded him to go with me to the bank. We put every cent of it into a savings account that he couldn’t touch for a year.

===Chinese===
他耸耸肩，一脸不以为然的神气。他有一张方方正正、老实巴交的脸，一双沉稳的眼睛，那种愤世嫉俗的神情配上他实在不太相称。我知道他是个正派小伙子，尽管自从到了我们这一段，他就一直在喝酒、耍酒疯。
吃过早饭，我们出去坐在太阳底下，那地方的木板人行道搭在一条沙沟上，成了一座小桥的样子。我和他长谈了一场。我提着那只装满他赢钱的旅行袋，终于说动他跟我一块儿去银行。我们把这笔钱一分不剩地存进了一个储蓄账户，一年之内他自己动不了它。

===Original===
From that night Blake and I were fast friends. He was the sort of fellow who can do anything for somebody else, and nothing for himself. There are lots like that among workingmen. They aren’t trained by success to a sort of systematic selfishness. Rodney had been unlucky in personal relations. He’d run away from home when he was a kid because his mother married again—a man who had been paying attention to her while his father was still alive. He got engaged to a girl down on the Southern Pacific, and she double-crossed him, as he said. He went to Old Mexico and let his friends put all his savings into an oil well, and they skinned him. What he needed was a pal, a straight fellow to give an account to. I was ten years younger, and that was an advantage. He liked to be an older brother. I suppose the fact that I was a kind of stray and had no family, made it easier for him to unbend to me. He surely got to think a lot of me, and I did of him. It was that winter I had pneumonia. Mrs. O’Brien couldn’t do much for me; she was overworked, poor woman, with a houseful of children. Blake took me down to his room, and he and the old Mexican woman nursed me. He ought to have had boys of his own to look after. Nature’s full of such substitutions, but they always seem to me sad, even in botany.

===Chinese===
从那天夜里起，布雷克和我就成了莫逆之交。他是那种能为旁人赴汤蹈火、却什么都替自己办不成的人。干力气活的里头这种人多了去了。他们没有被成功调教出一套有条不紊的自私。罗德尼在人与人的交情上一直不顺。他小时候就离家出走，因为他母亲改嫁了——那男人在他父亲活着的时候就开始巴结她。他在南太平洋铁路（Southern Pacific）那边和一个姑娘订了婚，那姑娘，按他的说法，把他坑了。他去了旧墨西哥（Old Mexico），让朋友把他全部积蓄投进一口油井，结果人家把他榨了个精光。他需要的是一个伙伴，一个正直的人好向他交代。我比他小十岁，这反而是个好处。他喜欢当哥哥。我想，我那种没人管、没家没业的身世，大概也让他更容易对我放下架子。他待我是真好，我待他也是。就在那年冬天，我得了肺炎。奥布莱恩太太（Mrs. O’Brien）没法怎么照料我；她那个可怜女人累得要死，一屋子的孩子。布雷克把我弄到他自己屋里去，他和那个老墨西哥女人一起护理我。他本该有自己的孩子好照看的。大自然里这样的顶替多的是，可在我眼里它们总叫人难过，连植物学里那样的情形也是。

===Original===
I wasn’t able to be about until spring, and then the doctor and Father Duchene said I must give up night work and live in the open all summer. Before I knew anything about it, Blake had thrown up his job on the Santa Fe, and got a berth for him and me with the Sitwell Cattle Company. Jonas Sitwell was one of the biggest cattle men in our part of New Mexico. Roddy and I were to ride the range with a bunch of grass cattle all summer, then take them down to a winter camp on the Cruzados river and keep them on pasture until spring.
We went out about the first of May, and joined our cattle twenty miles south of Pardee, down toward the Blue Mesa. The Blue Mesa was one of the landmarks we always saw from Pardee—landmarks mean so much in a flat country. To the northwest, over toward Utah, we had the Mormon Buttes, three sharp blue peaks that always sat there. The Blue Mesa was south of us, and was much stronger in colour, almost purple. People said the rock itself had a deep purplish cast. It looked, from our town, like a naked blue rock set down alone in the plain, almost square, except that the top was higher at one end. The old settlers said nobody had ever climbed it, because the sides were so steep and the Cruzados river wound round it at one end and undercut it.

===Chinese===
直到开春我才能下地走路，那时医生和迪谢纳神父（Father Duchene）都说我必须放弃夜里的活儿，整个夏天都得在露天里过活。我还没回过神来，布雷克就把圣菲（Santa Fe）铁路上的差事辞了，给他和我都在西特韦尔养牛公司（Sitwell Cattle Company）谋了个位子。乔纳斯·西特韦尔（Jonas Sitwell）是我们新墨西哥这一带最大的养牛人之一。罗迪（Roddy）和我那年夏天要赶着一群吃草的牛在牧区里转悠，然后再把它们赶到克鲁萨多斯河（Cruzados river）畔的一个冬令营去，在牧场上喂养到开春。
我们五月初出发，在帕迪以南二十英里、再往蓝色台地（the Blue Mesa）方向去的地方和我们的牛群会合。蓝色台地是我们从帕迪总能望见的几个地标之一——在一片平展展的地方，地标意义重大。往西北方，朝犹他（Utah）那一边，有摩门孤峰（Mormon Buttes），三座尖尖的蓝峰，老坐在那儿不动。蓝色台地在我们南边，颜色浓得多，几乎是紫的。人们说那岩石本身就透着深紫色。从我们镇上看过去，它像一块光秃秃的蓝色岩石，孤零零地立在平原上，几乎是个方块，只是顶端一头比另一头高些。老资格的拓荒者说从来没人爬上去过，因为四面太陡，而且克鲁萨多斯河绕着它的一头流过，把岩壁底下都掏空了。

===Original===
Blake and I knew that the Sitwell winter camp was down on the Cruzados river, directly under the mesa, and all summer long, while we drifted about with our cattle from one water-hole to another, we planned how we were going to climb the mesa and be the first men up there. After supper, when we lit our pipes and watched the sunset, climbing the mesa was our staple topic of conversation. Our job was a cinch; the actual work wouldn’t have kept one man busy. The Sitwell people were good to their hands. John Rapp, the foreman, came along once a month in his spring-wagon, to see how the cattle were doing and to bring us supplies and bundles of old newspapers.
Blake was a conscientious reader of newspapers. He always wanted to know what was going on in the world, though most of it displeased him. He brooded on the great injustices of his time; the hanging of the Anarchists in Chicago, which he could just remember, and the Dreyfus case. We had long arguments about what we read in the papers, but we never quarrelled. The only trouble I had with Blake was in getting to do my share of the work. He made my health a pretext for taking all the heavy chores, long after I was as well as he was. I’d brought my Caesar along, and had promised Father Duchene to read a hundred lines a day. Blake saw that I did it—made me translate the dull stuff aloud to him. He said if I once knew Latin, I wouldn’t have to work with my back all my life like a burro. He had great respect for education, but he believed it was some kind of hocus-pocus that enabled a man to live without work. We had *Robinson Crusoe* with us, and Roddy’s favourite book, *Gulliver’s Travels*, which he never tired of.

===Chinese===
布雷克和我都知道西特韦尔公司的冬令营就在克鲁萨多斯河边、台地的正下方，所以整个夏天，我们赶着牛从一个水坑漂荡到另一个水坑的时候，一直在盘算着怎么爬上那座台地、做头一批上去的人。吃过晚饭，我们点上烟斗看日落的时候，爬台地便是我们聊不完的老话题。我们这差事轻巧得很；真正的活儿让一个人干都闲得慌。西特韦尔公司对伙计们不薄。工头约翰·拉普（John Rapp）每个月坐着他的弹簧马车来一趟，看看牛群怎么样，再给我们送来补给和一捆捆旧报纸。
布雷克看报看得很认真。他总想知道这世上在发生些什么，尽管多半的事都叫他看不顺眼。他老是闷闷地想着他那个时代的大冤案：在芝加哥处死那几个无政府主义者的事——他还勉强记得——还有德雷福斯案（Dreyfus case）。我们常常为报上的事争得面红耳赤，可从来没真正拌过嘴。我跟布雷克唯一的别扭，是想抢着干我那一份活儿。他把我的身体当作借口，把所有重活都揽了过去——早在我身体和他一样结实很久以后还是这样。我随身带着我的恺撒（Caesar），答应过迪谢纳神父每天读一百行。布雷克盯着我要做到——逼着我把那些干巴巴的东西大声译给他听。他说我要是一旦学会了拉丁文，就不至于一辈子像头毛驴（burro）似的靠脊背吃饭了。他极其敬重学问，可又信着学问是某种戏法，能让一个人不干活也活得下去。我们带着《鲁滨逊漂流记》（*Robinson Crusoe*），还有罗迪最心爱的那本《格列佛游记》（*Gulliver's Travels*），他百读不厌。

===Original===
Late in October, Rapp, the foreman, came along to accompany us down to the winter camp. Blake stayed with the cattle about fifteen miles to the east, where the grass was still good, and Rapp and I went down to air out the cabin and stow away our winter supplies.

===Chinese===
十月底，工头拉普来了，要陪我们南下冬令营。布雷克留在东边大约十五英里外陪牛群，那边的草还算好；拉普和我则先下去把小屋通通风、把过冬的补给归置好。

### II / II

===Original===
The cabin stood in a little grove of piñons, about thirty yards back from the Cruzados river, facing south and sheltered on the north by a low hill. The grama grass grew right up to the doorstep, and the rabbits were running about and the grasshoppers hitting the door when we pulled up and looked at the place. There was no litter around, it was as clean as a prairie-dog’s house. No outbuildings, except a shed for our horses. The hillside behind was sandy and covered with tall clumps of deer-horn cactus, but there was nothing but grass to the south, with streaks of bright yellow rabbit-brush. Along the river the cottonwoods and quaking asps had already turned gold. Just across from us, overhanging us, indeed, stood the mesa, a pile of purple rock, all broken out with red sumach and yellow aspens up in the high crevices of the cliffs. From the cabin, night and day, you could hear the river, where it made a bend round the foot of the mesa and churned over the rocks. It was the sort of place a man would like to stay in forever.

===Chinese===
小木屋坐落在一小片矮松（piñon）林里，离克鲁萨多斯河（Cruzados river）约莫三十码远，朝南，北面有一道低矮的山岗替它挡着风。格兰马草（grama grass）一直长到门槛跟前，我们赶车到了、打量这地方的时候，野兔正四处乱跑，蚱蜢蹦到了门上。四下没有一点杂物，干净得像草原犬鼠的窝。除了一个拴马的棚子，再没别的附属屋舍。屋后的山坡是沙土地，一丛一丛高高的鹿角仙人掌（deer-horn cactus）盖满了；可南边除了草什么也没有，只一道道鲜黄的兔刷草（rabbit-brush）。河两岸的三角叶杨（cottonwoods）和白杨（quaking asps）已经转成了金黄。就在我们对岸——简直可以说是悬在我们头顶上——矗立着那座台地（mesa），一整堆紫色的岩石，崖壁高处的裂缝里尽是红的漆树（sumach）和黄的山杨。从屋里，日日夜夜，你都听得见河水——它在台地脚下拐了个弯，在石头上翻滚咆哮。这种地方，人巴不得一辈子待下去。

===Original===
I helped Rapp open the wooden shutters and sweep out the cabin. We put clean blankets on the bunks, and stowed away bacon and coffee and canned stuff on the shelves behind the cookstove. I confess I looked forward to cooking on an iron stove with four holes. Rapp explained to me that Blake and I wouldn’t be able to enjoy all this luxury together for a time. He wanted the herd kept some distance to the north as long as the grass held out up there, and Roddy and I could take turn about, one camping near the cattle and one sleeping in a bed.

“There’s not pasture enough down here to take them through a long winter,” he said, “and it’s safest to keep them grazing up north while you can. Besides, if you bring them down here while the weather’s so warm, they get skittish, and that mesa over there makes trouble. They swim the river and bolt into the mesa, and that’s the last you ever see of them. We’ve lost a lot of critters that way. The mesa has been populated by runaways from our herd, till now there’s a fine bunch of wild cattle up there. When the wind’s right, our cows over here get the scent of them and make a break for the river. You’ll have to watch ’em close when you bring ’em down.”

===Chinese===
我帮拉普（Rapp）推开木百叶窗，把屋里扫干净。我们在铺位上铺好干净的毯子，把咸肉、咖啡和罐头食品一一收进炉子后面的架子上。我承认，能在一口四眼铁炉子上做饭，我心里是盼着的。拉普跟我交代，布雷克和我一时半会儿还不能一块儿享受这番舒服。他想让牛群（herd）先待在北边一段距离的地方，只要那边的草还吃得住；我和罗迪（Roddy）轮班，一个在牛群旁边扎营，一个睡床铺。

「这儿下面的草场不够，养不过一整个长冬，」他说，「趁还能放，把它们留在北边吃草最稳妥。再说，天这么热的时候把它们赶下来，它们容易受惊（skittish），那边那座台地就惹麻烦。它们游过河，一头钻进台地去，那就再也见不着了。我们就这么丢了不少牲口（critters）。台地上住的全是从我们牛群里跑走的，到现在上头已经有挺不错的一大群野牛了。风向对的时候，我们这边的母牛闻得见它们的味儿，就撒开蹄子往河那边冲。等你们把它们赶下来，可得盯紧了。」

===Original===
I asked him whether nobody had ever gone over to get the lost cattle out.

Rapp glared at me. “Out of that mesa? Nobody has ever got into it yet. The cliffs are like the base of a monument, all the way round. The only way into it is through that deep canyon that opens on the water level, just where the river makes the bend. You can’t get in by that, because the river’s too deep to ford and too swift to swim. Oh, I suppose a horse could swim it, if cattle can, but I don’t want to be the man to try.”

I remarked that I had had my eye on the mesa all summer and meant to climb it.

“Not while you’re working for the Sitwell Company, you don’t! If you boys try any nonsense of that sort, I’ll fire you quick. You’d break your bones and lose the herd for us. You have to watch them close to keep them from going over, I tell you. If it wasn’t for that mesa, this would be the best winter range in all New Mexico.”

===Chinese===
我问他，难道就没人过去把走失的牛弄出来过。

拉普瞪着我。「从那座台地里弄出来？至今还没人能进去过呢。四周的崖壁就像一座纪念碑的基座，一圈全是那样。唯一的入口是那条直通到水面的深峡谷，就在河流拐弯的地方。可你也进不去，因为河水太深，没法蹚，又太急，没法游。哦，我想，要是牛能游过去，马大概也游得过去，可我不想当那个去试的人。」

我说，我整个夏天都盯着那座台地看，一心想爬上去。

「你给西特韦尔公司（Sitwell Company）干活的时候，想也别想！你们小子要是敢动这种糊涂念头，我立马把你们开了。你们会摔断骨头，还把牛群给我们丢了。我告诉你们，一定得盯紧了，别让它们跑过去。要不是那座台地，这儿就是全新墨西哥（New Mexico）顶好的冬季牧场了。」

===Original===
After the foreman left us, we settled down to easy living and fine weather; blue and gold days, and clear, frosty nights. We kept the cattle off to the north and east and alternated in taking charge of them. One man was with the herd while the other got his sleep and did the cooking at the cabin. The mesa was our only neighbour, and the closer we got to it, the more tantalizing it was. It was no longer a blue, featureless lump, as it had been from a distance. Its skyline was like the profile of a big beast lying down; the head to the north, higher than the flanks around which the river curved. The north end we could easily believe impassable﻿—sheer cliffs that fell from the summit to the plain, more than a thousand feet. But the south flank, just across the river from us, looked accessible by way of the deep canyon that split the bulk in two, from the top rim to the river, then wound back into the solid cube so that it was invisible at a distance, like a mouse track winding into a big cheese. This canyon didn’t break the solid outline of the mesa, and you had to be close to see that it was there at all. We faced the mesa on its shortest side; it was only about three miles long from north to south, but east and west it measured nearly twice that distance. Whether the top was wooded we couldn’t see﻿—it was too high above us; but the cliffs and canyon on the river side were fringed with beautiful growth, groves of quaking asps and piñons and a few dark cedars, perched up in the air like the hanging gardens of Babylon. At certain hours of the day, those cedars, growing so far up on the rocks, took on the bluish tint of the cliffs themselves.

===Chinese===
工头走了以后，我们便安顿下来，过起舒坦的日子，赶上好天气；白天蓝澄澄、金灿灿，夜里清清朗朗，降着白霜。我们把牛群放在北边和东边，轮流照看。一个人跟着牛群，另一个就在屋里睡觉、做饭。那座台地是我们唯一的邻居，我们越靠近它，它就越撩拨人心。它再也不是远处望去时那蓝蓝的、光秃秃的一团了。它的天际线像一头卧着的大兽的侧影：兽头朝北，比那两条被河水环绕的腰腹还要高。北端我们很容易相信是无法攀越的——陡直的崖壁从山顶一直落到平原，足有一千多英尺。可南面的腰腹，就在河对岸，看起来倒似乎上得去：一条深峡谷把整座山体劈成两半，从顶上的边缘一直裂到河边，再往这块实心的方块里蜿蜒回去，远远望去根本看不见，像一条老鼠道钻进了一块大奶酪。这条峡谷并没有打破台地那浑然一体的轮廓，你非得走到近处，才看得出它在那儿。我们正对着台地最短的一边；它南北不过三英里来长，东西却有将近两倍那么宽。顶上长没长树，我们看不见——它在我们头顶太高了；可河这边的崖壁和峡谷边上，却缀着一簇簇好看的草木：一片片的白杨和矮松，还有几棵黑黝黾的雪松，高高地悬在半空，活像巴比伦的空中花园。一天里有些时辰，那些长在如此高处的岩石上的雪松，会染上崖壁本身那种蓝幽幽的颜色。

===Original===
It was light up there long before it was with us. When I got up at daybreak and went down to the river to get water, our camp would be cold and grey, but the mesa top would be red with sunrise, and all the slim cedars along the rocks would be gold﻿—metallic, like tarnished gold-foil. Some mornings it would loom up above the dark river like a blazing volcanic mountain. It shortened our days, too, considerably. The sun got behind it early in the afternoon, and then our camp would lie in its shadow. After a while the sunset colour would begin to stream up from behind it. Then the mesa was like one great ink-black rock against a sky on fire.

===Chinese===
那上面天亮得比我们这儿早得多。天蒙蒙亮我起身，下到河边去打水，我们这营地还又冷又灰，可台地的顶上已经被日出照得通红，岩石边那些细细瘦瘦的雪松全成了金色——是金属那样的金，像失去光泽的金箔。有几个早晨，它在黑沉沉的河水上方浮现出来，像一座熊熊燃烧的火山。它也把我们的白天缩短了不少。午后刚过不久，太阳就躲到了它背后，于是我们的营地便躺在它的影子里。过一会儿，落日的颜色开始从它背后往上涌。那时台地就像一大块墨黑的岩石，衬着一片燃烧的天空。

===Original===
No wonder the thing bothered us and tempted us; it was always before us, and was always changing. Black thunderstorms used to roll up from behind it and pounce on us like a panther without warning. The lightning would play round it and jab into it so that we were always expecting it would fire the brush. I’ve never heard thunder so loud as it was there. The cliffs threw it back at us, and we thought the mesa itself, though it seemed so solid, must be full of deep canyons and caverns, to account for the prolonged growl and rumble that followed every crash of thunder. After the burst in the sky was over, the mesa went on sounding like a drum, and seemed itself to be muttering and making noises.

===Chinese===
怪不得这东西老搅扰我们、勾引我们；它老在我们眼前，老在变样。黑色的雷雨常常从它背后翻滚过来，像一头黑豹毫无预兆地朝我们扑下。闪电在它四周游走，又刺进它身体里去，我们总担心它会把那片灌木点着。我从没听见过哪儿打雷打得那样响。崖壁把雷声朝我们反弹回来，我们便想，这座台地虽说看上去是实心的，里头一定满是深深的峡谷和洞穴——不然每一次雷声炸过之后，那绵延不绝的低吼和轰隆就没法解释了。天上那一炸过去之后，台地还像鼓一样咚咚作响，仿佛它自己在嘟嘟囔囔、弄出声响。

===Original===
One afternoon I was out hunting turkeys. Just as the sun was getting low, I came through a sea of rabbit-brush, still yellow, and the horizontal rays of light, playing into it, brought out the contour of the ground with great distinctness. I noticed a number of straight mounds, like plough furrows, running from the river inland. It was too late to examine them. I cut a scrub willow and stuck a stake into one of the ridges, to mark it. The next day I took a spade down to the plantation of rabbit-brush and dug around in the sandy soil. I came upon an old irrigation main, unmistakable, lined with hard smooth cobbles and ’dobe cement, with sluices where the water had been let out into trenches. Along these ditches I turned up some pieces of pottery, all of it broken, and arrowheads, and a very neat, well-finished stone pickax.

That night I didn’t go back to the cabin, but took my specimens out to Blake, who was still north with the cattle. Of course, we both knew there had been Indians all over this country, but we felt sure that Indians hadn’t used stone tools for a long while back. There must have been a colony of pueblo Indians here in ancient times: fixed residents, like the Taos Indians and the Hopis, not wanderers like the Navajos.

===Chinese===
有天下午我出去打火鸡。太阳快要落山的时候，我穿过一大片兔刷草，还是黄澄澄的，水平的阳光斜斜地照进去，把地面的起伏照得分外清晰。我注意到有一些笔直的土垄，像犁沟一样，从河边一直往内陆延伸。天太晚了，来不及细看。我折了一根矮柳枝，插在其中一道土脊上做个记号。第二天，我带了把铲子下到那片兔刷草丛里，在沙土里挖了起来。我挖到了一条古老的灌溉主渠，错不了——渠底铺着又硬又光的鹅卵石和土坯（’dobe）水泥，还有放水入沟的闸口。沿着这些沟渠，我翻出了一些陶器的碎片——全碎了——还有箭头，和一把十分精巧、做得相当讲究的石鹤嘴锄。

那天夜里我没有回木屋，而是把发现的几样东西拿出去给布雷克看——他还留在北边守着牛群。我们俩当然都知道，这一带从前到处都是印第安人，可我们心里有数，印第安人早就有很久不用石器了。古时候这里一定有过一支普韦布洛印第安人（pueblo Indians）的聚落：是定居下来的居民，像陶斯印第安人（Taos Indians）和霍皮人（Hopi）那样，而不是像纳瓦霍人（Navajo）那样到处游荡。

===Original===
To people off alone, as we were, there is something stirring about finding evidences of human labour and care in the soil of an empty country. It comes to you as a sort of message, makes you feel differently about the ground you walk over every day. I liked the winter range better than any place I’d ever been in. I never came out of the cabin door in the morning to go after water that I didn’t feel fresh delight in our snug quarters and the river and the old mesa up there, with its top burning like a bonfire. I wanted to see what it was like on the other side, and very soon I took a day off and forded the river where it was wide and shallow, north of our camp. I rode clear around the mesa, until I met the river again where it flowed under the south flank.

On that ride I got a better idea of its actual structure. All the way round were the same precipitous cliffs of hard blue rock, but in places it was mixed with a much softer stone. In these soft streaks there were deep dry watercourses which could certainly be climbed as far as they went, but nowhere did they reach to the top of the mesa. The top seemed to be one great slab of very hard rock, lying on the mixed mass of the base like the top of an old-fashioned marble table. The channels worn out by water ran for hundreds of feet up the cliffs, but always stopped under this great rimrock, which projected out over the erosions like a granite shelf. Evidently, it was because of this unbroken top layer that the butte was inaccessible. I rode back to camp that night, convinced that if we ever climbed it, we must take the route the cattle took, through the river and up the one canyon that broke down to water-level.

===Chinese===
对于像我们这样离群索居的人来说，在一片荒野的土地里发现人类劳作和经营过的痕迹，是件叫人心头一动的事。它像一道讯息传到你这里，让你对你每天踩着的这片土地生出不同的感觉。这片冬季牧场比我到过的任何地方都更中我的意。每天早晨我从木屋门里出来去打水，总要为我们这暖和齐整的小窝、为这条河、为上头那座古老的台地——它的顶上烧得像一堆篝火——生出崭新的欢喜。我想看看它另一边是什么样子，没过多久便腾出一天，在我们营地北边那段河面又宽又浅的地方蹚水过了河。我骑马绕着台地整整走了一圈，一直走到河水从它南麓下面流出来的地方，又和河相遇。

那一趟跑下来，我对它真实的结构有了更清楚的认识。四面一圈都是同样陡峭的、坚硬的蓝色岩石崖壁，不过有些地方夹杂着一种软得多的石头。这些软的石层里，有些又深又干的水沟，顺着它们确实能爬上一段，可哪一条也通不到台地的顶上。顶上看上去像一大块极硬的整石板，盖在底下那驳杂的基座之上，活像一张老式大理石桌子的桌面。被水冲刷出来的沟壑顺着崖壁往上有好几百英尺，可总是在这道巨大的檐岩底下断了——那檐岩像一道花岗岩的架子，突出来盖在那些被侵蚀的沟壑之上。显然，正是因为这一层完整无缺的盖顶，这座方山才上不去。那天夜里我骑马回营地，心里认定：我们哪天真要爬上去，非得走牛走的那条路不可——从河里穿过去，再顺着那条一直裂到水面的峡谷上去。

### III / III

===Original===
We brought the bunch of cattle down to the winter range in the latter part of November. Early in December the foreman came along with generous provisions for Christmas. This time he brought with him a supercargo, a pitiful wreck of an old man he had picked up at Tarpin, the railroad town thirty miles northeast of us, where the Sitwells bought their supplies. This old man was a castaway Englishman, Henry Atkins by name. He had been a valet, and a hospital orderly, and a cook, and for many years was a table steward on the Anchor Line. Lately he had been cooking for a sheep outfit that were grazing in the cattle country, where they weren’t wanted. They had done something shady and had to get out in a hurry. They dropped old Henry at Tarpin, where he soon drank up all his wages. When Rapp picked him up there, he was living on handouts.
“I’ve told him we can’t pay him anything,” Rapp explained. “But if he wants to stay here and cook for you boys till I make my next trip, he’ll have plenty to eat and a roof over him. He was sleeping in the livery stable in Tarpin. He says he’s a good cook, and I thought he might liven things up for you at Christmas time. He won’t bother you, he’s not got any of the mean ways of a bum﻿—I know a bum when I see one. Next time I come down I’ll bring him some old clothes from the ranch, and you can hire him if you want to. All his baggage is that newspaper bundle, and there’s nothing in it but shoes﻿—a pair of patent leathers and a pair of sneakers. The important thing is, never, on any account, go off skylarking, you two, and leave him with the cattle. Not for an hour, mind you. He ain’t strong enough, and he’s got no head.”

===Chinese===
十一月下旬，我们把那一群牛赶到了冬季牧场。十二月初，工头来了，带着丰盛的圣诞用品。这一回他带来了一个搭脚的——一个叫人可怜的老废物，是他在塔平（Tarpin）捡到的。塔平是我们东北方三十英里开外的铁路小镇，西特韦尔家（Sitwells）就在那儿办货。这老头是个流落异乡的英国人，名叫亨利·阿特金斯（Henry Atkins）。他当过贴身男仆，当过医院的护理员，当过厨子，好多年都在锚行（Anchor Line）的轮船上做餐厅侍者。近来他在一帮放羊的人那儿做饭——那帮人在养牛的地界放羊，本就不受人待见，又干了点什么见不得人的勾当，匆匆忙忙只好滚蛋。他们把老亨利扔在了塔平，没过多久他就把工钱全喝光了。拉普（Rapp）在那儿把他领走的时候，他正靠人家的施舍过活。
「我跟他讲明了，我们一个子儿也付不起，」拉普解释说，「可要是他愿意留在这儿替你们俩做饭，等我下一趟来，那他吃的管够，还有个遮风挡雨的屋顶。先前他在塔平的马厩里过夜。他说自己是个好厨子，我想他兴许能让你们圣诞节热闹点儿。他不会给你们添乱的，他身上一点也没有那种二流子的坏毛病——二流子我一眼就认得出。下回我下来，从牧场给他带几件旧衣裳来，你们要愿意就雇下他。他全部的家当就是那个报纸打的包袱，里头除了鞋什么也没有——一双漆皮鞋，一双胶底帆布鞋。最要紧的一桩：你们俩无论如何不许撇下牛群出去撒野。一刻钟也不行，记着。他身子骨不顶事，又没个主见。」

===Original===
Life was a holiday for Blake and me after we got old Henry. He was a wonderful cook and a good housekeeper. He kept that cabin shining like a playhouse; used to dress it all out with piñon boughs, and trimmed the kitchen shelves with newspapers cut in fancy patterns. He had learned to make up cots when he was a hospital orderly, and he made our bunks feel like a Harvey House bed. To this day that’s the best I can say for any bed. And he was such a polite, mannerly old boy; simple and kind as a child. I used to wonder how anybody so innocent and defenceless had managed to get along at all, to keep alive for nearly seventy years in as hard a world as this. Anybody could take advantage of him. He held no grudge against any of the people who had misused him. He loved to tell about the celebrated people he’d been steward to, and the liberal tips they had given him. There with us, where he couldn’t get at whisky, he was a model of good behaviour. “Drink is me weakness, you might say,” he occasionally remarked apologetically. He shaved every morning and was as clean as a pin. We got to be downright fond of him, and the three of us made a happy family.

===Chinese===
得了老亨利之后，布雷克和我的日子简直像过节。他是个顶呱呱的厨子，又是个把家好手。他把那间小木屋拾掇得亮堂堂的，像座玩具屋似的；还常用矮松（piñon）的枝桠给它装点一番，又拿剪出花样的旧报纸给厨房的搁板镶边。他当医院护理员那会儿学过铺床，经他一拾掇，我们的铺位躺上去竟像哈维旅馆（Harvey House）的床那样舒服。时至今日，我要夸一张床，至多也就夸到这个地步。而且他是那么一个客气、有礼数的老伙计，心思单纯，心肠又软，像个孩子。我常纳闷，这么一个毫无心机、全无防备的人，究竟是怎么在这般狠硬的世道里挨过了将近七十个年头、活了下来。谁都能欺负他。可那些亏待过他的人，他一个也不记仇。他顶爱讲他从前侍候过的那些名流，讲他们赏的小费多么大方。跟我们在一块儿，够不着威士忌，他便是个规规矩矩的模范。「酒嘛，可以说是我的一个弱点，」他不时这样抱歉似的说一句。他每天早晨刮脸，浑身上下干净得挑不出一点碴。我们渐渐地真把他当自家人疼起来，我们仨成了一户快快活活的人家。

===Original===
Ever since we’d brought our herd down to the winter camp, the wild cattle on the mesa were more in evidence. They came down to the river to drink oftener, and loitered about, grazing in that low canyon so much that we began to call it Cow Canyon. They were fine-looking beasts, too. One could see they had good pasture up there. Henry had a theory that we ought to be able to entice them over to our side with salt. He wanted to kill one for beefsteaks. Soon after he joined us we lost two cows. Without warning they bolted into the mesa, as the foreman had said. After that we watched the herd closer; but a few days before Christmas, when Blake was off hunting and I was on duty, four fine young steers sneaked down to the water’s edge through the brush, and before I knew it they were swimming the river﻿—seemed to do it with no trouble at all. They frisked out on the other side, ambled up the canyon, and disappeared. I was furious to have them steal a march on me, and I swore to myself I’d follow them over and drive them back.

The next morning we took the herd a few miles east, to keep them out of mischief. I made some excuse to Blake, cut back to the cabin, and asked Henry to put me up a lunch. I told him my plan, but warned him not to bear tales. If I wasn’t home when Blake came in at night, then he could tell him where I’d gone.

===Chinese===
自从我们把牛群赶到冬营，台地（mesa）上的那群野牛便越发显眼了。它们更勤地下到河边来饮水，还在那条浅峡谷里四处溜达吃草，赖在那儿不肯走，久而久之我们便管那地方叫牛峡（Cow Canyon）。那一群也都是些体面的好牲口。一看便知，那上头有的是好草。亨利有一套说法：我们该能用盐把它们引到我们这边来。他想杀一头，吃牛排。他来了没多久，我们就丢了两头母牛。它们连个招呼也不打，一头扎进了台地——正像工头说的那样。打那以后我们把牛群盯得更紧了；可圣诞节前几天，布雷克出去打猎，正轮着我当班，四头上好的小公牛顺着灌木丛偷偷摸摸地蹭到水边，我还没回过神来，它们已经游过了河——简直毫不费力。它们在对岸蹦跶着上了岸，碎步小跑着进了峡谷，一晃眼不见了。叫它们这么抢了先，我气得够呛，暗暗发誓非要跟过去、把它们赶回来不可。

第二天早上，我们把牛群往东赶了几英里，免得它们再惹祸。我编了个由头支开布雷克，折回木屋，请亨利给我包一顿午饭。我把我的打算告诉了他，可叮嘱他别多嘴。要是我到夜里布雷克回来时还没到家，那时他再把我的去处告诉他。

===Original===
Henry went down to the river with me to watch me across. It had grown colder since morning, and looked like snow. The old man was afraid of a storm; said I might get snowed in. But I’d got my nerve up, and I didn’t want to put off making a try at it. I strapped my blanket and my lunch on my shoulders, hung my boots around my neck to keep them dry, stuffed my socks inside my hat, and we waded in. My horse took the water without any fuss, though he shivered a good deal. He stepped out very carefully, and when it got too deep for him, he swam without panic. We were carried downstream a little by the current, but I didn’t have to slide off his back. He found bottom after a while, and we easily made a landing. I waved goodbye to Henry on the other side and started up the canyon, running beside my horse to get warm.

===Chinese===
亨利陪我下到河边，看着我过河。自早上起天又冷了些，像是要下雪的模样。老头怕碰上风暴，说我别给雪困在了里头。可我那股子劲已经上来了，不想再拖。我把毯子和午饭绑在肩上，把靴子挂在脖子上好不让它沾水，袜子塞进帽子里，便下了水。我的马下水时一点不带含糊，虽说它哆嗦得厉害。它一脚一脚踩得很仔细，等水没过了它，它便不慌不忙地游了起来。我们被水流往下游冲了一截，可我并不用从它背上滑下去。过了一阵它踩到了底，我们顺顺当当地上了岸。我在对岸朝亨利挥了挥手道别，便顺着峡谷往上跑起来，在马旁边一路小跑着好暖暖身子。

===Original===
The canyon was wide at the water’s edge, and though it corkscrewed back into the mesa by abrupt turns, it preserved this open, roomy character. It was, indeed, a very deep valley with gently sloping sides, rugged and rocky, but well grassed. There was a clear trail. Horses have no sense about making a trail, but you can trust cattle to find the easiest possible path and to take the lowest grades. The bluish rock and the suntanned grass, under the unusual purple-grey of the sky, gave the whole valley a very soft colour, lavender and pale gold, so that the occasional cedars growing beside the boulders looked black that morning. It may have been the hint of snow in the air, but it seemed to me that I had never breathed in anything that tasted so pure as the air in that valley. It made my mouth and nostrils smart like charged water, seemed to go to my head a little and produce a kind of exaltation. I kept telling myself that it was very different from the air on the other side of the river, though that was pure and uncontaminated enough.

===Chinese===
峡谷在水边一带很宽阔，虽说它拐着急弯、拧着麻花似的往台地深处钻，却始终是这般开阔宽敞的模样。其实它是一条很深的山谷，两侧山坡缓缓地斜上去，嶙峋多石，却长满了草。有一条清楚的小径。马是不懂得挑路的，可牛你尽管信得过——它们总能找着最省脚力的道，专走坡度最缓的地方。那蓝灰色的岩石和被日头晒透的枯草，映在那一天少见的紫灰色天幕底下，使整条山谷染上了一种极柔和的颜色，淡紫，夹着浅金，于是那天早上偶尔长在巨石旁边的几株雪松，看上去便黑沉沉的。也许是因为空气里隐隐有要下雪的意思，我只觉得，我从前呼吸过的任何东西，都没有这条山谷里的空气这般纯净。它呛得我口鼻发辣，像苏打水冒泡似的，又似乎微微冲上了我的脑门，叫我生出一种昂扬之感。我一路走一路对自己说，这和河对岸的空气是两回事——虽说那边也够干净、够没受玷污的了。

===Original===
When I had gone up this canyon for a mile or so, I came upon another, opening out to the north﻿—a box canyon, very different in character. No gentle slope there. The walls were perpendicular, where they weren’t actually overhanging, and they were anywhere from eight hundred to a thousand feet high, as we afterward found by measurement. The floor of it was a mass of huge boulders, great pieces of rock that had fallen from above ages back, and had been worn round and smooth as pebbles by the long action of water. Many of them were as big as haystacks, yet they lay piled on one another like a load of gravel. There was no footing for my horse among those smooth stones, so I hobbled him and went on alone a little way, just to see what it was like. My eyes were steadily on the ground﻿—a slip of the foot there might cripple one.

===Chinese===
我顺着这条峡谷走了大约一英里光景，便撞见了另一条峡谷，朝着北面敞开——那是一条匣子峡谷（box canyon），性情与前一条全然不同。这里再没有什么缓坡。崖壁都是直上直下的，有的地方甚至向外倒悬，据我们日后测量，高度从八百到一千英尺不等。谷底是一片巨石的堆垒，都是不知多少年代以前从上方崩落的岩块，被流水长年累月地磨得像卵石一样圆滑。其中许多大得像一座座草垛，却互相叠压着，仿佛一堆碎石。这些光溜溜的石头上我的马没法落脚，我便把它拴住，独自再往前走了一小段，只想看看前头是什么光景。我的两眼紧紧盯着脚底下——在那地方脚底一滑，兴许就能叫人残废。

===Original===
It was such rough scrambling that I was soon in a warm sweat under my damp clothes. In stopping to take breath, I happened to glance up at the canyon wall. I wish I could tell you what I saw there, just as I saw it, on that first morning, through a veil of lightly falling snow. Far up above me, a thousand feet or so, set in a great cavern in the face of the cliff, I saw a little city of stone, asleep. It was as still as sculpture﻿—and something like that. It all hung together, seemed to have a kind of composition: pale little houses of stone nestling close to one another, perched on top of each other, with flat roofs, narrow windows, straight walls, and in the middle of the group, a round tower.

===Chinese===
这一路攀爬乱石，不一会儿我贴身的湿衣裳底下就腾起了一身热汗。我停下来喘口气，偶然一抬头，朝崖壁望去。但愿我能把那一刻我在那上头看见的，原原本本说给你听——就在那个头一个早晨，透过一层纷纷扬扬、轻轻落下的雪帘。在我头顶很高很高的地方，约莫一千英尺的光景，嵌在崖壁一张巨大的石洞里，我看见了一座小小的石头城，睡在那里。它静得像一尊雕塑——而且也确有几分像雕塑。它浑然一体，仿佛自有它的章法：一座座灰白的小石屋彼此紧紧依偎，层层叠叠地摞在一起，平的屋顶，窄窄的窗子，笔直的墙，而在这群屋子的正中，是一座圆塔。

===Original===
It was beautifully proportioned, that tower, swelling out to a larger girth a little above the base, then growing slender again. There was something symmetrical and powerful about the swell of the masonry. The tower was the fine thing that held all the jumble of houses together and made them mean something. It was red in colour, even on that grey day. In sunlight it was the colour of winter oak-leaves. A fringe of cedars grew along the edge of the cavern, like a garden. They were the only living things. Such silence and stillness and repose﻿—immortal repose. That village sat looking down into the canyon with the calmness of eternity. The falling snowflakes, sprinkling the piñons, gave it a special kind of solemnity. I can’t describe it. It was more like sculpture than anything else. I knew at once that I had come upon the city of some extinct civilization, hidden away in this inaccessible mesa for centuries, preserved in the dry air and almost perpetual sunlight like a fly in amber, guarded by the cliffs and the river and the desert.

===Chinese===
那座塔的比例美极了，在离基座不远的地方微微鼓出一圈更粗的腰身，再往上又重新收细。那砌石的弧线里有一种匀称而有力的东西。正是这座塔，是那根定海的神针，把那一堆杂乱的屋宇拢在一起，让它们有了意义。它是红的，哪怕在那样一个灰蒙蒙的日子也是红的。在阳光底下，它是冬日橡树叶的颜色。一排雪松沿着石洞的边缘长着，像一座花园。它们是那里头唯一活着的生灵。那样的静、那样的寂、那样的安息——一种不朽的安息。那座村落就这么俯瞰着峡谷，带着一种永恒的从容。纷纷落下的雪花，洒在矮松上，给它平添了一种特别的庄重。我说不出来。它比什么都更像一尊雕塑。我当下就明白，我撞见的是某一个已然绝灭的文明所留下的城，在这座人迹罕至的台地里藏了不知多少个世纪，在干燥的空气和几乎终年不歇的日照里保存下来，像琥珀里的一只蝇，由崖壁、河流和沙漠一同守着。

===Original===
As I stood looking up at it, I wondered whether I ought to tell even Blake about it; whether I ought not to go back across the river and keep that secret as the mesa had kept it. When I at last turned away, I saw still another canyon branching out of this one, and in its wall still another arch, with another group of buildings. The notion struck me like a rifle ball that this mesa had once been like a beehive; it was full of little cliff-hung villages, it had been the home of a powerful tribe, a particular civilization.

===Chinese===
我站在那里仰望它，心里却踌躇起来：这事我该不该就连布雷克也告诉他呢？我该不该回到河对岸去，把这个秘密守住，就像台地一直守着它那样？等我终于转身要走的时候，我又看见从这条峡谷里还分出另一条峡谷，而在它崖壁上，又是一个石洞，又有一群落落在一起的屋宇。一个念头像步枪子弹一样击中了我：这座台地从前一定像一座蜂房；它里头密密麻麻全是这种悬在崖壁上的小村落，它曾是一个强悍的部族、一个独特的文明的家园。

===Original===
That night when I got home Blake was on the riverbank waiting for me. I told him I’d rather not talk about my trip until after supper﻿—that I was beat out. I think he’d meant to upbraid me for sneaking off, but he didn’t. He seemed to realize from the first that this was a serious matter to me, and he accepted it in that way.

After supper, when we had lit our pipes, I told Blake and Henry as clearly as I could what it was like over there, and we talked it over. The town in the cliffs explained the irrigation ditches. Like all pueblo Indians, these people had had their farms away from their dwellings. For a stronghold they needed rock, and for farming, soft earth and a water main.

“And this proves,” said Roddy, “that there must have been a trail into the mesa at the north end, and that they carried their harvest over by the ford. If this Cow Canyon was the only entrance, they could never have farmed down here.” We agreed that he should go over on the first warm day, and try to find a trail up to the Cliff City, as we already called it.

===Chinese===
那天夜里我到家时，布雷克正在河岸上等我。我跟他说，我这会儿宁愿先不谈这一趟——我累瘫了。我看他是本想责备我偷偷溜出去的，可他没有。他似乎一开始就明白，这事对我是顶要紧的，也就以那种态度认下了它。

吃过晚饭，我们点上了烟斗，我便尽力把那边是什么光景，原原本本讲给布雷克和亨利听，三个人一起商量起来。崖壁上那座城，正好替那些灌溉渠作了注解。和所有的普韦布洛印第安人（pueblo Indians）一样，这些人当年也是把田地开在离住处远远的地方。要筑一座堡垒，他们需要岩石；要种庄稼，则需要松软的泥土和一条水源。

「这也证明了，」罗迪说，「台地北头当年一定有一条路通进去，他们是蹚着浅滩把收成运过来的。要是这条牛峡是唯一的入口，他们决不可能在下头这一带种地。」我们商量定，头一个暖和日子他便过去，想法找一条上到崖城（Cliff City）去的路——我们那时已经这么叫它了。

===Original===
We talked and speculated until after midnight. It was Christmas eve, and Henry said it was but right we should do something out of the ordinary. But after we went to bed, tired as I was, I was unable to sleep. I got up and dressed and put on my overcoat and slipped outside to get sight of the mesa. The wind had come up and was blowing the squall clouds across the sky. The moon was almost full, hanging directly over the mesa, which had never looked so solemn and silent to me before. I wondered how many Christmases had come and gone since that round tower was built. I had been to Acoma and the Hopi villages, but I’d never seen a tower like that one. It seemed to me to mark a difference. I felt that only a strong and aspiring people would have built it, and a people with a feeling for design. That cluster of buildings, in its arch, with the dizzy drop into empty air from its doorways and the wall of cliff above, was as clear in my mind as a picture. By closing my eyes I could see it against the dark, like a magic-lantern slide.

===Chinese===
我们谈着、揣想着，一直谈到过了半夜。那天是平安夜，亨利说我们理当做点什么不同寻常的事儿。可上床之后，我虽累极了，却怎么也睡不着。我爬起来，穿好衣裳，披上大衣，悄悄溜到外头，好望一望那座台地。起风了，一阵阵的乱云被风赶着掠过天幕。月亮将近圆了，正悬在台地的正上方，在我看来，台地还从来没有那样庄严、那样寂静过。我心里盘算着，自从那座圆塔造起来，到如今过了多少个圣诞节了。我到过阿科马（Acoma），到过霍皮人（Hopi）的村庄，可见过的塔里没有一座像那一座。我觉得它意味着一种不同。我感到，只有一个强悍而心怀远志的民族才会造出它来，一个对造型有感觉的民族。那一簇屋宇，嵌在它的石洞里，门外便是令人目眩的、直直坠入空谷的悬崖，头顶又是直立的崖壁——这一整幅景象在我脑海里清晰得像一幅画。只要一闭上眼，它便浮现在那片黑暗之中，像一张幻灯片（magic-lantern slide）。

===Original===
Blake got over the river before New Year’s day, but he didn’t find any way of getting from the bottom of the box canyon up into the Cliff City. He felt sure that the inhabitants of that sky village had reached it by a trail from the top of the mesa down, not from the bottom of the canyon up. He explored the branch canyons a little, and found four other villages, smaller than the first, placed in similar arches.

These arches we had often seen in other canyons. You can find them in the Grand Canyon, and all along the Rio Grande.
